   DETERMINATION OF THE REID VAPOR PRESSURE OF PETROLEUM PRODUCTS
1.    PRINCIPLE
      This method provides for air partial saturation or petro­
leum products with a Reid vapor pressure below 26 lb using a
Reid Vapor Pressure Bomb.     The gasoline chamber portion of the
vapor pressure apparatus is filled with a chilled sample and
connected to the air chamber.       The apparatus is immersed in a
constant temperature bath (100 ±0.2°F) and shaken periodically,
until equilibrium is reached.
2.    APPARATUS-
      2.1     Refrigerator or Freezer                                    --.,
      2.2     Reid Vapor Pressure Bomb.   This unit is equipped with
a 4 1~ •• Oto 15 psig gauge (Fig. ~II-6).
      2.3     Constant temperature water bath (100 ~0.20F)
3.    ANALYTICAL PROCEDURE
      3.1     Handling or Sample
      3.1.1    The size or the sample container used 1n taking the
sample shall not be less than 1 quart nor more than 1 gallon.
The container shall    be a solvent can, with a 1 3/4 in. lid opening,
and filled to approximately 90% capacity.
      3.1.2    In all cases after a sample has been taken, the con-.
tainer shall not be opened unless it has been cooled t~ <320F.
      3.1.3    Samples shall be put 1n ice as soon as taken, and
transferred to the laboratory for immediate storage in the refri­
gerator, and stored there until the tests have been completed.           -
                               66
Samples in leaky containers shall not be considered for tests
but shall be discarded and new samples taken.
      3.2     Preparation tor Test
      3.2.1    Air Saturation of Sample in Sample Container -
Remove the sample container from the refrigerator, unseal it
examine it for its liquid content, which shall be approximately
90 percent of the container capacity.    After the liquid content
has been assured, reseal the container, shake vigorously and
and return it to the refrigerator.
      3.2.2    Preparation of the Gasoline Chamber - Place the open
gasoline chamber and the sample transfer connection into the
refrigerator for sufficient time to allow the chamber and connect­
ion to reach temperature (32°F to 40°F).
      3.2.3    Preparation of Air Chamber (lOOOF Procedure) -
After rinsing and purging the air chamber, connect the gauge-to
the air chamber.    Immerse the air chamber to at least 1 in. above
its top in the water bath maintained at 100 :0.20F for not less
than 10 minutes just before coupling it to the gasoline chamber.
Do not remove the air chamber from the bath until the gasoline
chambe~ has been filled as described in Section 3.3.
      3.3   Sample Transfer - With all in readiness, remove the
chilled sample from the refrigerator, uncap it and insert the
chilled transfer connection and air tube (Fig. III-5).
Then place the empty chilled gasoline chamber over the sample
delivery tube of the transfer connection.    Invert the entire
system rapidly so that the gasoline chamber is finally in an up­
right position with the delivery tube extending to within 1/4 in.


                               67
of the bottom of the gasoline chamber.      Fill the gasoline chamber
to overflowing.     Lightly tap the sasoline chamber against the
 work bench to insure that the sample is tree of air bubbles.       If
any sample is displaced, refill the chamber to overflowing.
      3.3.1 Assembly of Apparatus - Without delay and as quickly
as possible, attach the air chamber to the gasoline chamber.
      3.3.2     Introduction of Apparatus to Bath - Turn the assembled
vapor pressure apparatus upside down to allow the sample in the
gasoline chamber to run into the air chamber and shake vigorously
in a direction parallel to the length of the apparatus.       Immerse
the assembled apparatus into the bath, maintained at 100 %0.2°F,
in an inclined position so that the connection or the gasoline
a.~d air chamber is below the water level and may be observed close­
ly for leaks.     If no leaks are observed, immerse the apparatus to
at least 1 in. above the top of the air chamber.      Observe the
apparatus for leaks throughout the test.      If a leak is detected,
discard the test.
      3. 3.3    Measurement .of Vapor Pressure - After the assembled
vapor pressure apparatus has been immersed in the bath for 5
minutes, tap the pressure gauge lightly and record the reading.
Withdraw the apparatus from the bath, invert it, shake it vigorous­
ly and immediately place back in the· bath.    At intervals
of about 10 minutes, repeat this agitation and gauge observation
at least five times, until the last two gauge readings are constant.
These operations normally require 60 to 90 minutes.     Read the fjnal
gauge pressure to the nearest 0.05 psig for gauges with intermediate
graduations of 0.1 psig and to the nearest 0.1 psig for gauges with

                                68
_graduation of 0.2 to 0.5 psig.    The value obtained is the Reid
Vapor Pressure or the sample under test.
      4.    A nomograph Figs. III-7 and III-8) is used to convert
Reid Vapor Pressure to true vapor pressure (See Reference 4.2).
4.    REFERENCES
      4.1   Vapor Pressure of Petroleum Products (Reid Method)
ASTM designation D-323-72, Book 23 ASTM Petroleum Products (1977).
      4.2   Nomograph of Petroleum Products Vapor Pressure.
Air Pollution Eng~neering Manual, 2nd Ed., AP40, 635-636.

        DETERMINATION OF TRIMETHYLAMINE IN EFFLUENTS
l.    PRINCIPLE
      The trimethylamine is absorbed and converted to the stable
hydrochloride salt in dilute hydrochloric acid solution.          The
trimethylamine is regenerated by addition of sodium hydroxide
and determined by gas chromatography using flame ionization
detection.
                              •
2•    APPARATUS
      2.1     Gas Chromatograph.       This unit equipped with a flame
ionization detector,must be capable or detecting 2 µgm/ml of tri­
methylamine with a peak height of at least 3 times the noise level.
      2.1.1     A glass 4 1 x 1/8" analytical column is used, contain­
ing 15% Dowfax 9N9 + 7.5% TEPA on Chromosorb W 60/80 mesh.          The
material is .prepared, packed and conditioned employing standard
techniques.
      2.2     10 µl syringe
      2.3     Injection port fitted with a glass sleeve
3.    REAGENTS
      3.1 Hydrochloric Acid (O.l N).          Add 8.4 ml or concentrated
hydrochloric acid to 500 ml distilled water and dilute to 1 liter.
      3.2     Sodium Hydroxide Solution (1 N).      Dissolve 40 g of
sodium hydroxide pellets in 500 ml or distilled water and dilute
to 1 liter.
      3.3     Litmus Paper - Red




                                  70
4.     ANALYTI~AL PROCEDURE
       4.1   Measure and record the total liquid volume of the
impingers.    If samples are to be analyzed within 3 days proceed as
in ~.2 to 4.4. If analysis is delayed keep samples refrigerated.
       4.2   Transfer a 9.0 ml aliquot or the sample to a 25 ml
graduated test tube.    Add 1 ml of 1 N sodium hydroxide, sufficient
to make the solution basic.    Cover the test tube with parafilm;
invert and mix well.    Check to insure the sample is basic with
                                    •
pH paper.
      4.3    Inject a 2 ~l sample into the gas chromatograph in­
jection port.    The operating parameters are: column temperature
750c; detector temperature 2000c; injection port temperature 160°c; - ..-..
carrier gas 40 ml/min; hydrogen 20 ml/min and air 250 to 300 ml/min.
      4.4    Using peak heights, calculate.the tr1methylam1ne
concentration compared to the peak heights of the standard solu-
tions of tr1methylam1ne.

      5.1    Standard Stock Amine Solution.   To a 1 liter volumetric
flask, add 0.1691 g of trimethylam:1.ne hydrochloride salt,
dissolve and bring to volume with 0.1 N hydrochloric acid.      This
standard solution contains 100 ~gm tr1methylam1ne/ml.
      5.2    Working Standard Solution.   Dilute the standard stock
solution with O.l N hydrochloric acid to make working standards
containing 2, 5 and 10 1,1gm tr1methylam1ne/ml.   These.standards are
processed in the same manner as the samples described in Section 4.1.

     STANDARDIZATION OF CARBON DIOXIDE CALIBRATION GAS
1.    RATIONALE
      Carbon dioxide standards used tor calibrating source test
instrumentation must be checked periodically for accuracy and
quality assurance purposes.
2.    PRINCIPLE
      The carbon dioxide gas is absorbed by Ascarite and the in­
crease in weight of the absorbent is determined gravimetrically.
The carbon dioxide gas must first pass through Drierite to remove
water vapor, which would otherwise interfere with the determination.
3.    APPARATUS
      3.1   Analytical balance
      3.2   Schwartz Absorption Combustion Tubes.   These tubes are
available from Kontes Glass Co. (Cat. No. KB91250).    The tubes
are filled with the appropriate material and plugged at each end
with glass wool.
                                                                    \
      3.3   Wet Test Meter (readout in liters)
      3.4   Rotameter Oto l liter/min range
      3.5   Two Stage Regulator, with a controlling valve
4.    REAGENTS
      4.1   Anhydrone
      4.2   Ascarite
      4.3   Drierite (8 to 14 mesh)
5.   :A,NALYTJ;_CAL PROCEDURE
      5.1   Assemble the sampling train as shown in Figure III-9.
The train consists or 2 U-shaped Schwartz Combustion absorption



                                 73
tubes attached to the carbon dioxide standard cyl~nder equipped
with a 2 stage regulator and controlling valve.        The first
absorption tube contains Drierite to remove water vapor which
may be present in the cylinder gas.        The inlet of the second
absorption tube contains Ascarite and the other halt Anhydrone,
(separated by a glass wool plug) to remove respectively the carbon
dioxide, and any water formed during the collection reaction.
      5.2   Prior to the analysis, flush the entire sampling train
with approximately 2 liters of the calibration gas.
      5.3   Disconnect the Ascarite-Anhydrone tube from the sample
train and carefully weigh with an analytical balance.        Record the
weight (W1).   Reconnect the Ascarite-Anhydrone tube to the
sampling train.
      5.4   Record the wet test meter reading (M1).       The cylinder
controlling valve is slowly opened, and using the rotometer as
a guide, the flow is adjusted as rapidly as possible to 0.5 to
1 liter per minute.
      5.5   The volume of sample depends on the concentration or
carbon dioxide in the sample.        The gain in weight of the Ascarite­
Anhydrone tube should be at least 20 mg.        As a guide, 1000 ppm
carbon dioxide should have a sample size of about 10 liters.
      5.6   After sufficient sample has been collected, close the
controlling valve.    The Ascarite-Anhydrone tube is disconnected
and again carefully weighed (W2).       The final wet test meter
reading is then recorded (M2).




                                74
      5.7   At least two determinations are to be made and
must agree to within ±5% of the mean.
6.    CALCULATION

      1 mg carbon dioxide per liter is equal to 556 ppm
      PPM CO2     c   (W2 - W1) (556)
                      (M2 ~ M1)

7.    REFERENCE
      7.1   Textbook of Quantitative Inorganic Analysis,
Kolthoff and Sandell, 3rd Ed. 1952, MacMillan, N.Y.

     STANDARDIZATION OF CARBON MONOXIDE CALIBRATION GAS
1.    RATIONALE
      Carbon monoxide standards used ~o calibrate source test
instrumentation I:1Ust be checked periodically for accuracy and
quality assurance purposes.
2.    PRINCIPLE
      Carbon monoxide calibration gas is oxidized to carbon
dioxide by use of Hopcal1te catalyst.     The formed carbon dioxide
is absorbed by the A~carite and is determined grav1metr1cally.
Any water vapor and carbon dioxide 1n the sample must be removed
prier to the Hopcal1te catalyst.
3.    APPARATUS
      3.1   Analytical balance
      3.2   Rotameter, Oto l liter range
      3.3   Wet Test Meter (readout in liters)
      3.4   Two stage regulator, with a controlling valve
      3.5   Schwartz Absorption Combustion Tubes.    These tubes
are available from Kontes Glass Co. (Cat. No. K891250). The
tubes are filled with the appropriate material and plugged at
each end with glass wool.
      3.6   Boiling water bath
      3.7 Hopcalite U-trap.      This trap is constructed of 7 to 10
mm ID borosilicate glass and 1s approximately 20 cm in length.
The trap is filled with 3 to 6 g of Hopcalite and plugged
at each end with glass wool.     The trap should be fitted with        -
spherical joints for ease of assembling and disassembling.

                                 76
                                 •,
      4.    REAGENTS
            4.1     Anhydrone
            4.2     Ascarite
            4.3     Hopcalite, Active (4 to 20 mesh, available from the
      Mine Safety Appliance Co.)
            4.4     Drierite (8 to 14 mesh)
      5.    ANALYTICAL PROCEDURE
            5.1     Assemble a sampling train as shown in Figure III-10.     The
      train consists of a Drier!te trap to remove moisturej an Ascarite
      trap to remove carbon dioxidej Hopcalite trap, immersed in boiling
      water bath, a Drierite trap to remove the generated moisture and
      a final Ascarite-Anhydrone trap to collect the carbon dioxide
      formed in the oxidation process.        A wet test meter and a rota-
'-'   meter complete the train.
            5.2 The Hopcalite catalyst must be conditioned prior to
      each determination.       This is accomplished by disconnecting the
      sampling train after the Hopcalite trap and allowing approximately
      15 liters of the calibration gas to pass through at approximately
      0.5.liter/min with the Hopcalite trap immersed in the boiling
      water bath.
            5.3 While conditioning the Hopcalite catalyst, carefully
      weigh the Ascarite-Anhydrone trap with an ~alytical balance.
      Record the weight (W1).
            5.4     After conditioning, reconnect the sampling·train.
            5.5     Record the original wet test meter reading (M1).
      By means of the controlling valve on the 2 stage regulator,adjust
      the flow through the sampling train to approximately 0.5 liter

                                       77
per minute as rapidly as possible using the rota.meter as an
indicator.           The gain in weight or tbe Ascar1te-Anhydrone tube
should be at least 20 mg.                       The sample time will depend on the
concentration or the carbon monoxide 1n the sample.                        As a guide,
200 ppm or carbon monoxide should have a sample size or approximate­
ly 60 liters.
      5.6 .After sufficient sample has been collected, close the
controlling valve.                       The Ascar1te-Anhyd.rc~e tube 1s ~iscoMected
and again carefully weighed (W2).                       The final wet test meter
reading is then recorded (M2).
      5.7 At least two determinations are to be made and
must agree to within t5% or the mean.
6.    CALCULATIONS

         STANDARDIZATION OF HYDROCARBON CALIBRATION GASES
1.       RATIONALE
         Hydrocarbon gas standards used for calibrating source test
instrumentation must be checked periodically for accuracy and
quality assurance purposes.           These standards usually consists of
a single hydrocarbon normally meth~e, propane or hexane.
2.       PRINCIPLE
         The hydrocarbon calibration gas concentration is deten:uned
by preparing two laboratory standards, one higher and one lower than
the expected cylinder concentration.              Gas chromatography is then
used to determine the cylinder gas concentration by comparison
wit~ prepared laboratory standards.
3.       APPARATUS
         3.1     Gas Chromatograph.        This unit is equipped with a gas
sampling valve, a flame ionization detector and necessary accessory
gases.
         3.2     Analytical columns.       Any column may be used which is
capable of resolving the hydrocarbons of interest such as :
         3.2.1    For methane, a 6 rt 1/8" Chromosorb 102, 80 to 100
mesh column is normally used.
         3.2.2    For propane, a 6 rt 1/8" Chromosorb 102, 80 to 100
mesh column is normally used.
         3.2.3    For hexane, a 6 rt 1/~" silicone L-~5 on firebrick
60 to 80 mesh is normally used.


                                      79
       3.3   Stainless Steel (SS) Tanks.   Use 4 to 34 liter tanks,
calibrated for volume which have been previously steam cleaned,
to be hydrocarbon free and fitted with a vacuum-pressure gauge,
valve and nipple.
      3.4    Syringes.   Use 5, 10, and 30 ml calibrated syringes.

by injecting the hydrocarbon from the syringe through a rubber

       STANDARDIZATION OF HYDROGEN SULFIDE CALIBRATION GAS
1.     RATIONALE
       Hydrogen sulfide standards used for source test instrumenta­
tion must be checked periodically for accuracy and quality
assurance purposes.
2.     PRINCIPLE
       Hydrogen su~fide is absorbed from a gas stream by a solution
of cadmium sulfate forming a precipitate of cadmium sulfide.        The
collected sulfide is oxidized by an excess of standard iodine
solution which is back titrated with standard sodium thiosulfate.
3.     APPARATUS                                                          .--.
       3.1    Greenburg Smith Impingers
       3.2    Rotameter, Oto 10 liter/min range
       3. 3   Dry Test Meter (readout in cub·1c feet)
       3.4    50 ml burettes
       3.5 Two stage regulator with a controlling valve
ll •   REAGENTS

       ~.l    Cadmium sulfate solution.   Add 11.2 g of anhydrous
cadmium sulfate to l liter of distilled water.
       ~.2    Hydrochloric Acid, (6 N).   Dilute concentrated hydro­
chloric acid 1:1 with distilled water.
       ~-3    Standard Sodium Thiosulfate Solution, (O.l N).
Dissolve 15.8 g of anhydrous sodium thiosulfate in l liter of
distilled water.     Add lg of sodium carbonate as a preservative.
Allow to stand for a day before standardizing.


                                83
       4.4   Starch Solution - 1% Indicator.       Available from any
chemical supply house.
       4.5   Iodine Solution (O.l N KI3).       Place 12.7 g of reagent
grade iodine (I2) in a 500 ml beaker, add 40 g of potassium iodide
and 250 ml of distilled water.         Stir periodically.   When all the
iodine is completely dissolved, dilute to approximately 1 liter.
Keep the solution in a dark glass stoppered bottle and store in
a cool place.
      4.6    Arsenic Trioxide
      4.7    Sodium Bicarbonate
      4.8    Sodium Hydroxide (1 N).       Dissolve 40 g of sodium
hydroxide pellets in250ml of distilled water and dilute to 1 liter.

with teflon tubing. Fig~ III-11 details the sampling t~a!r..

concentration of the hydrogen sulfide in the cylinder.         As a guide,
100 ppm hydrogen sulfide should have a sample size of approximate­
ly 300 liters.

agree to within :5% of the mean.

      STANDARDIZATION OF NITRIC OXIDE CALIBRATION GAS
1.    RATIONALE
      Nitric oxide standards used for source test instrumentation
must be checked periodically for accuracy and quality assurance
purposes.
2.    PRINCIPLE
      The nitric oxide is absorbed ir. alkalir.e permanganate solution
with Greenburg Smith impingers fitted with semi-fine frits.        The
nitrite and nitrate ions thus formed are determined by first
converting the nitrite formed during the collection to nitrate.
The total nitrate is then reduced to nitrite, which is determined
by a chromatropic acid indicator.
3.    APPARATUS
      3.1   Greenburg Smith Impingers.     The impingers are fitted
with semi-fine frits (70 porosity), available from Ace Glass Cc.
(Cat. #7201-04) or equivalent.
      3.2   50 ml graduated Erlenmeyer flasks with screw caps
      3-3   Two Stage Regulator with a controlling valve
      3.4   Wet Test Meter (readout in liters)
      3-5   Hot Plate
      3.6   Spectrophotometer
      3.7   Rotameter Oto 1 liter/min range
4.    REAGENTS
      4.1   Absorbing Solution.    Dissolve 40 g of K."11104 and 20 g
NaOH pellets in 400 ml of distilled water and dilute to 1 liter


                                  87
      with distilled water.
             4.2    Oxalic Acid Solution.    Dissolve Bog of oxalic acid
      and dilute to 1 liter with distilled water.
            4.3     Sulfuric Acid (1.0 N).   Add 27.6 ml of concentrated
      sulfuric acid to 500 ml of dfstilled water and dilute to 1 liter.
            4.4     Nitrate Reagents. Nitra Ver III (Cat .• #14065) and
      Nitra Ver VI (Cat. 114119) are available from Hach Chemical Co.,
      Ames, Iowa.
      5.    SAMPLING PROCEDURE
            5.1     The sa~pling train consists of 3 Greenburg SI:lith
      1mpingers modified with semi-fineglass frits, each containing 100
      ml of absorbing solution as shown in Fig.III-12.      The impingers are
      connected in series followed by a wet test meter and rotameter.
      The sampling train is connected to the standard gas cylinder by
      means o~ teflon tubing.
            5.2     Record the original wet test meter reading (M1).      By

      means of the controlling valve on the 2 stage regulator, adjust the
      flow through the sampling train to approximately 0.5 liter per
      minute as rapidly as possible, using the rotameter as an indicator.
      The sample time will depend on the concentration of the nitric
      oxide in the cylinder.     As a guide, 100 ppm of nitric oxide requi~es
      a sample size of approximately 20 liters.
            5.3    After sufficient sample has been collected shut off the
      controlling valve.  Record the final wet test meter reading (M 2 ),
      and remove the imp1ngers from the sampling train (Fig. III-12).
            5.4    At least 2 determinationsare to be made and the results
'-"   must agree to within ±5% of the mean.

                                      88
6.       ANALYTICAL PROCEDURE
         6.1   Measure and record the liquid volume of each impinger.
Transfer an aliquot, not to exceed 3.0 ml for analysis,.containing
Oto      60 ~gm of nitrite.   The aliquot may require dilution with
unreacted absorbing solution or may be used directly.        If preferred,
all three impingers may be combined to form a composite sample,
thereby requiring only a single analysis.
         6.2   The aliquot taken is transferred to a 5o·m1 Erlenmeyer
flask fitted with a screw cap.        A reagent blank of 2.0 ml is also
prepared from the absorbing solution which was used for the sampling.
Acidify with l.O N sulfuric acid, using l ml of acid per ml or
                                               /
aliquot.
         6.3   Heat the sample on a hot plate to 50° to 6ooc, and add
1 ml of oxalic acid per ml of aliquot, let stand until colorless.
Should more oxalic acid be required to reduce the permanganate,
add dropwise so as not to have more than 3 drops in excess.       Cool
to room temperature and dilute to the 30 ml mark with distilled
water.
         6.~   Add to the sample and the blank, the content of a Nitra
Ver VI pillow, cap the flask and shake for exactly 5 minutes.
Allow the sample to stand for 30 seconds and transfer to a graduated
test tube.     Add the content of a Nitra Ver III pillow, cover the
test tube with parafilm and mix for one minute.       Let stand tor 10
minutes and read the formed pink color at 500 nm,-using a 13 mm
cuvette, setting the blank at 100% transmittance.       Obtain ~gm N02
in the sample from a standard curve.

                                 LAB 21
REF: Regs. 8-3-301, 8-3-302

     DETERMINATION OF COMPLIANCE FOR AIR-DRIED ARCHITECTURAL
                     WATER BASED COATINGS


1.      PRINCIPLE
        A weighed sample of water based architectural coating is
distilled to insure complete separation of the water component
from the coating.      The distillate is then diluted with methanol
and the water content.determined by a gas chromatograph using a
thermal-conductivity detector. The Poropak Q column separates
water from any volatile organics present, water being eluted
first from the column.
 2.     APPARATUS
       •2.1    Gas Chromatograph.    This unit is equipped with a thermal
conductivity detector and a glass sleeve injection port .

        2.2    Analytical Column.    Use a 6• x 1/4" O.D. column

dish with a flat bottom.
        2.6    Drying Oven (1100:1°c) ·
        2.7    Glas-col Heater
        2.8    Powerstat
        2.9    Analytical Balance
        2.10    Disposable Beral Pipettes.   These are available f~on Curtir.
Matheson Co. (Cat. No. 376-970).

6.    PREPARATION OF THE STANDARD CURVE

      6.1   Using methanol as the diluent, make up a series of water
standards in 25 ml volumetric flasks to contain: 0.5, l.0,
5.0, 10.0 and 15.0 % W/W water.
      6.2   Using a 10 µ1 syringe, inject 2 )J1 or each standard
into the gas chromatograph.   From the resulting chromatograms
construct a standard curve by plotting the average recorder
peak height of triplicate analyses versus% water (W/W) on a
rectilinear graph paper.   The curve 1s linear to approximately
25% W/W water.

REF: Regs. 8-3-301, 8-3-302
     DETERMINATION OF COMPLIANCE FOR AIR-DRIED ARCHITECTURAL
                        OIL EASED COATINGS


1.      PRINCIPLE
        A weighed    sample of paint or related product is refluxed
with toluene to determine the water content of the sample.             The
app~atus consists of a glass flask, heated by suitable means
and provided with a reflux condenser discharging into a trap con­
nected to the flask.        The trap serves to collect and measure the
condensed water.        The voletile content or the coating is determined
on a separate aliquot of the sample.           This method is applicable
only to oil based paints containing less than 2% W/W water.
2.     • APPARATUS

        2.l   Refluxing unit as shown in Fig. III-15
        2.2   Powerstat ( 0 to 110 volts)
        2.3   Analytical Balance
        2.~   Drying Tube.    A glass     drying tube of the calcium
chloride type.
        2.5   Aluminum Foil Dish.       Use a 58 mm diameter x 10 mm high
dish with a flat bottom.       The dish is available from Curtin
Matheson Co. (Cat. No. 079-053).
        2.6   Drying Oven (ll0°i1°c)
        2.7   Triple Beam Balance, tO.l g capability
        2.8   Disposable Beral Pipette.       These are available from Curtin
Matheson Co. (Cat. No. 376-970)~
3.      REAGENTS
        3.1   Toluene
        3.2   Calcium chloride

      4.1     Determination of Total Volatile Content
      4.1.1' Weigh accurately 0.4 to 0.6 g (±0.0001 g) or paint

toluene by swirling the dish until the sample is even distributed
at the bottom of the dish.        Dry the sample in the oven at ll0±1°c
for two hours.     Cool the sample in a desiccator and weigh.      Run
samples in duplicate.        Result~ should not vari by more than 5%.

REF: Reg. 8-4-111
  DETERMINATION OF VOLATILE WEIGHT LOSS OF POLYESTER RESINS
-1.    PRINCIPLE
      A pour pan method is used, where       a weighed amount of
polyester resin is reacted with methyl ethyl ketone peroxide        (MEKP)

catalyst to form a 4" x 1/4" thick casting.       The volatility loss
is determined at room temperature (75±3°F) over a three hour
period, with samples having a gel time between 15 to 20 minutes.
      This method is applicable only to polyester resins and
does not simulate spray booth operations.
2.    APPARATUS
      2.1     Analytical balance
      2.2     4" x 1/2" can lid or equivalent.    The lid must be
capable of containing about 100 cc volume to which an accurate
a.mount of catalyst can be added.
      2.2.1     Stirring rod, glass or metal
      2.3     Clock or timer
      2.4     8 oz wide mouth bottles
3.    REAGENT
      3.1     Polyester Resin
      3.2     Methyl Ethyl Ketone Peroxide   (MEKP)

4.    DETERMINA1ION OF GEL TIME
      4.1   To a tared 8 oz wide mouth bottle (2" in diameter),
containing a glass or metal stirring rod, add 100 g (±0.l g)
of polyester resin.


                                104
                CONDENSER




FIG,   I11-15
P£FLUX UNIT
          4.2     To the polyester resin,add 1.0 g (:0.1 g) of MEKP.
Record the time and thoroughly mix fer 30 seconds.
          4.3     Periodically mix the contents until it commences to
thicken.        When the resin thickens, dip and remove the stirrer
four times a minute until the resin strings or snaps from the
stirrer.        This is-the gel time.     Record the time to the nearest
minute.          Note:    If the gel time does net correspond to 15 to
20 minutes, a· catalyst adjustment is necessary for the sample to
conform to the required gel ti.me.         If the gel time is between
25 to 20 minutes proceed with Section 5.
          4.4    Gel Time Adjustment.     A catalyst adjustment is necessary
whenever the gel time varies . from the appropriate 15 to 20 minutes .
Additonal catalyst lowers the gel time.          Variations in relative gel
times with different catalyst additions are listed below and serve
as a guide fer obtaining the proper gel time.

          % MEKP Catalyst Added                 Approximate Gel Time
                   1.00                              40 minutes
                   1.50                              30 minutes
                   2.00                              20 minutes
          If for example a sample using 1% W/W MEKP catalyst has a
gel time of 40 minutes, an additional 1.0% MEKP catalyst should.
reduce the gel time to approximately 20 minutes.
5.     DETERMINATION OF VOLATILE ORGANICS
       5.1       Tare weigh a 4" x 1/2" metal lid or equivalent, con­
taining a stirring rod to t0.01 g.          Record the weight as W1.




                                    105
              ~.2    Add approximately 50 ml (50 g) of polyester resin
     to the metal lid, to form a 1/4" thick casting.             Record the weight
     of the lid plus the polyester resin to t0.0l gas w2 .
              5.3    Add 0.5 g ±0.0l g of MEKP catalyst to the polyester
     resin.     Record weight as W3.     If a gel time adjustment was
     necessary, then the MEKP used must be in the same ratio to the
     polyester resin as was determined in Section 4.4.
              5.4    After addition of the catalyst record the time.
     Thoroughly mix the catalyst into the polyester resin- by means
     of the stirring rod for 30 seconds at room temperature (75±30~).
     Note:     The mixing and catalyzation volatility loss should be
     done in a hood or well ventilated area.
              5.5    Periodically weigh the polyester resin content to
     determine the wiehgt loss per unit time.           At least three weighings
-~
     should be made during the fi~st 30 minutes of the test.             Record
     all weight losses with the attendant time lapse as Wt·
              5.6    Continue the weight loss determinations for 3 hours,
     taking periodic temperature readings and weighings every 30
     minutes.       After 3 hours, record the final weight as Wr.
     Note:    Periodic weight loss readings are taken to give an
     indication of the rate loss of the volatile organic compounds.
     6.      CALCULATIONS
             6.l    To determine the percent loss of volatile organics
     after time t.
                                                   W3   -   Wt
                    % Volatile Organics Loss   •   ----- X         100

      REF: Regs. 10-1-301,

             DETERMINATION OF TOTAL FLUORIDE IN EFFLUENTS
      1.    PRINCIPLE
            Gaseous and water soluble particulate fluorides are collected
      in a dilute akaline solution.         The absorved fluorides are quantified
      by the use of a fluoride specific ion meter.        A buffer solution
      containing a chelating agent is used to prevent possible inter­
      ferences from aluminum, iron and hydroxyl ions.
      2.    APPARATUS
            2.1     Orion Specific Ion Meter (Model 407A)
            2.1.1     Sleeve-type Reference Electrode (Orion No. 90-01-00)
            2.1.2     Fluoride Ion Electrode (Orion No. 94-09)
            2.2     Magnetic stirrer and teflon stirring bar (2.5 cm)
'-"         2.3     Polyethylene beakers, 100 ml
            2.4     Polyethylene 1 liter volumetric flask
      3.    REAGENTS
            3.1     Sodium Fluoride
            3.2     Total Ionic ·Strength Adjustment Buffer (TISAB)
            3.2.1     Dissolve 228 ml of glacial acetic acid, 232 g of
      sodium chloride and 4 g of CYTA (1,2, cyclo-hexylene-dinitrilo)
      Tetraacetic Acid in a 4 liter beaker containing approximately
      2000 ml of distilled water.
            3.2.2    Slowly add, with stirring, 160 ml of 50% sodium
      hydroxide solution while cooling the beaker in an ice bath.
      Allow the solution to come to room temperature and adjust the pH
      to between 5.0 and 5.5 using 50% sodium hydroxide solution.


                                      108
      3.2.3     Transfer the solution into a 4 liter bottle and
bring to volume with distilled water.
      3.3     Sodium Hydroxide Solution (O.l N).   Dissolve 4.0 g
of sodium hydroxide pellets in l liter of distilled water.·
4.    ANAiYTICAL PROCEDURE
      4.1     Measure and record the total volumes of each impinger.
      4.2     Transfer a 15.0 ml aliquot of sample to a 100 ml
polyethylene beaker containing a teflon stirring rod, and add
15.0 ml of the TISAB solution.
      4.3     Calibra~e the ion meter as described in Section 6.
      4.4     Immerse the ion meter electrodes into the prepared
sample and stir the solution slowly.     Reocrd the µg fluoride
reading after a constant reading has been obtained.
      4.5     All sample readings must be bracketed between the
calibration standards.     If the sample reads less than the 1.0 µg
F standard, report the result as traces found.
      4.5.l    Sample readings greater than 10 µg F must be diluted
with a solution containing equal amounts of TISAB and 0.1 N
sodium hydroxide to be in the range of the calibration standards.
Alternately, a smaller aliquot of the impinger may be taken and
brought to 15.0 ml with 0.1 N sodium hydroxide.     Add 15.0 ml of
TISAB and proceed as in 4.4.
      4.6   The ion meter should be recalibrated periodically with
the middle range calibration standard.· Rechecking after
every tenth sample has been found to be adequate.


                                .
                               109
5.    STANDARD PREPARATION
      5.1     Standard Stock Fluoride Solution
      Weigh and transfer 0.2210 g of sodium fluoride to a 1 liter
volumetric flask.     Diszolve in distilled water and bring to
volume.     This solution contains 100 µg of fluoride ion per ml.
This solution is stable for one year when stored in a plastic
bottle.
      5.2     Working Stanaard Fluoride Solutions
      Three standards, respectively 1.0 1 5.0 and 10.0 µg F/ml
are prepared by the appropriate dilution of the standard stock
fluoride solution with distilled water.     These standards if
stored in plastic bottles are stable !or six months.
6.    CALIBRATION OF THE ION METER
      6.1     Prepare three calibration fluoride solutions contain­
ing 1.0 1 5.0 1 and 10.0 µgas follows:
      6.1.1     The 1.0 µg calibration standa~d is made by pipetting·
1.0 ml of the 1.0 µg F/ml standard (5.2) into a 100 ml polyethyle~e
beaker and adding 14.0 ml of 0.1 N sodium hydroxide.
      6.1.2     The 5.0 µg calibration standard is made by pipettir.g
0.5 ml of the 10.1 µg F/ml standard (5.2) into a 100 ml poly­
ethylene beaker and 14.5 ml of 0.1 N sodium hydroxide.
      6.1.3     The 10.0 µg calibration standard is made by pipetting
1.0 ml of tbe 10.0 µg F/ml standard (5.2) into a 100 ml polyethylene
beaker and adding 14.0 ml or 0.1 N sodium hydroxide.
      6.2     The !on meter is calibrated using the three calibration
standards (6.1.1 - 6.1.3), each with 15.0 ml of TISAB havi~g been
added, as described in the instrument manual.       It is important to
maintain a constant slow stirring rate when the electrodes are

                               110
immersed in the calibration solutions.            Rinse the electrodes and
                                                                                 .l
blot dry with clean tissue between readings to prevent solution
carry over.

              DETERMINATION OF HYDROGEN SULFIDE IN EFFLUENTS
    l.       PRINCIPLE
             The hydrogen sulfide is absorbed in an alkaline cadmium·
    hydroxide solution to form cadmium sulfide.        The precipitated
    cadmium sulfide is then dissolved in hydrochloric acid and
    absorbed in a known volume of iodine solution.         The iodine
    consumed is a measure or the hydrogen sulfide content of the
    effluent.
    2.       APPARATUS
             2.1   Buret, Oto 50 ml
             2.2   Iodine flask, 500 ml
    3.       REAGENTS
             3.1   Absorbing Solution.    Dissolve 4.3 g cadmium sulfate
    hydrate CdSO4.8H2O and 0.3 g of sodium hydroxide in 1 liter of
    distilled.water.      Mix the cadmium hydroxide suspension thoroughly
    before using the absorbing solution.
             3.2   Hydrochloric Acid Solution (10% W/W).    Dilute 230 ml
    of concentrated hydrochloric acid to 1 liter with distilled
    water.
             3.3   Iodine Solution (0.1 N).   Dissolve 24 g of potassium
    iodide in 300 ml of distilled water in a l liter graduated
    cylinder.      Weigh 12.7 g of resublimed iodine into a weighing
    bottle and transfer to the potassium iodide solution.        Mix until
    the iodine is completely dissolved.       Slowly dilute the solution
    to 1 liter with distilled water and transfer to a brown glass
    stoppered bottle.

                                    112
      3-~   Standard Iodine Soltuion (0.01 N).    Dilute 100 ml of
the 0.1 N iodine solution (3.3) in a volumetric flask to·1 liter
with distilled water.     Standardize before using as follows:
pipette 25.0 ml of the 0.01 N iodine solution into a 125 ml flask.
Titrate with stapdard 0.01 N thiosulfate solution to a light
yellow color.   Add a few drops of starch solution, and continue
the titration until the blue color just disappears.     From the
results of this titration, calculate the exact normality of
the iodine solution (5.1).
      3.5   Standard Thiosulfate Solution (0.1 N).    For each liter
of solution, dissolve 2~.8 g sodium thiosulfate hydrate
(Na2S203.5H20) and 0.10 g sodium carbonate in distilled water.
Standardize the solution as follows:     weigh and transfer
approximately 2 g of potassium dichromate (to the nearest .0001 g)
to a 500 ml volumetric flask.     Dissolve and dilute to volume with
distilled water.   Dissolve approximately 3 g of potassium iodide
in 50 I:1l of distilled water contained in an iodine flask; then
add 10 ml of 10% hydrochloric acid solution.     Pipette 50.0 ml
of the dichromate solution into the same iodine flask, swirl
once gently and allow to stand in.the dark for 5 minutes.       Add
approximately 150 ml of distilled water, swirl, and immediately
titrate with the thiosulfate solution to a light yellow color.
Add 2 ml of starch solution and titrate until the blue color
disappears and a pale green color remains.     From duplicate
titrations, calculate the exact normality of the sodium thio­
sulfate solution (5.2).

I. SOURCE TEST POLICY
         1.1 Introduction
The purpose of this volume of the Manual of Procedures rs to
advl~• persons subject to the Regulatlons of the Bay Area Air
Qaality Management District of the policy of the Air Pollution
Control Officer relative to source testing and to promulgate
procedures used to determine compllance with the regulations
of this District.


Source Test methodology Is a state of the art practice and
therefore occasional changes wi II be made to this volume.
A form has been provided In the front of this volume for those
who wish to receive these periodic changes.


The contents of this Manual, and Its ~ubsequent changes 1 are
subject to approval     by   the Board of Ci rectors of this District,
and therefore, become law as provided for In the Health and
Safety Code of the State        of California.


        1.2 Definitions: The detlnltioRs used herein are consistent
with those used In the regulations of the District.        The
definitions of type "A" and "B" sample points are expanded for
clari fl cation.
        1.2.2 Representative Sampllng: The over-riding factor for
all source tests shall be that every reasonable effort •ust be
made to obtain samples that are truly representative of the
source being ·tested for that given set of process variables.
In all cases accepted engineering practice shall be fol lowed for
al I test procedures.
          1.2.3 Sampling Points: The sampling point Is the locetion on
e source or Its releted control equipment where samples ere
teken.     For purposes of determining comp I lence with District
Regulations, samples taken et e sampling potnt shell be deemed
Identical to those et the point of emission to the atmosphere.
The Regulations address themselves to type "A" end "B" emission
points.


The primary consideration for locating e sample port shell
be that the sample port location accurately represents the nature,
extent, quantity and degree of the contaminant et the emissions
point.     Ports shell be located, tf reasonably possible, et e type
"A" sample point.


          1.2.3.1 Sempllng et type·"A" emission points.      A type "A"
sample point shell be located In a smooth stack et least eight stack
diameters downstream of eny bends, Inlets, constriction, flow
altering device or change of area or geometry end two diameters
upstream of the stack exit or other flow disturbance.         Fore
non-circular duct, the equivalent diameter will be determined from
the following equetlon:


                      Equivalent Diameter   •     length x width
                                                2 length x width


          1.2.3.2.Sampllng at type "B" •~lsslon Points. A type "B"
sample point means any sample point not ~uallfylng es a type ~A"
emission point.


The quantity of emission from a type "B" emission point shall be

                                   2
the quantity of emission computed ~y multipylng the quantity of
emission from a test area by the proportion which the whole area
bears to such test area.    Such test area may be taken as the cross
sectional area of the Inlet to a sample probe.     The emission from
any test area of a type "B" emission point shall be deemed to be
representative in every respect of the emissions from the entire
area.   Emissions from the test area may be measured at the place
and by the procedure which result In the highest measurement of air
contaminants.


        1.2.4 Sampl Ing Facl I !ties: Section 1-410 of Regulation I
places the burden of providing proper sampling facilities on the
person responsible for the emission.    The followlng are normally
conslde~ed necessary:


        1.2.4.1. Sample Ports:   For circular ducts, at least two ports
90 degrees apart on a plane perpendicular to the longltudinal
axis of the duct shall be Installed at the sample point.     For
circular ducts, ten :feet In diameter or greater, four ports
ninety degrees apart on a plane perpendicular to the longitudinal
axis shall be Installed.


        For non circular ducts, access shall be provided to the
entire cross-sectional area of the duct.


Each port, where possible, shall be a nominal three Jnch diameter
pipe with external tapered pipe threads.


Ports shall be Installed flush with the Interior stack wal I.


                                  3
                                                                       ··--
Gate valves should be lnstal led on sample ports only when extreme
stack conditions or the presence of hazardous materials require
such devices for safety considerations.


Questions concerning locations ·should be referred to the Chief of
Source Test.
       1.2.4~2 Sampling Area Clearance: There shall be sufficient
clearance In the vicinity of each test port to allow the Insertion
of a probe equal In length to the stack diameter.


       1.2.4.3 Safety: Sampling facilities, access ways and platforms
sh~II meet all applicable OSHA requirements for Industrial and
personal safety and conform to any other relevant safety guldllnes.


       1.2.5 Quality Assurance:    It Is absolutely essential In any
testing program that a quality assurance program be establ lshed
                                                                         -
and maintained.     The provlsl~ns of Air Monitoring Quality Assurance
Volume IV, Quality Assurance In the Testing of Stationary Sources,
California State Air Resources Board shall apply.


       1.2.5.1 Instrumental Methods: Specifications and procedures
for zero and span checks on analytical Instruments are In each test
procedure.     Where available, calibration gases shall be traceable
to National Bureau of Standards reference materials.    Confirmation
of their values shall be made by reference procedures at 1ntervals
of not more than three months.


       1.2.5.2 Recorders: Analog chart recorders must be
compatible with Instrument output.


                                  4
                1.2.5.3 Manual Methods: Sampling shall be conducted to compensate,
      where reasonably possible, for significant stratification and non­
      homogeneity In the gas stream, so that the sample of the gas stream
      approaches a representative sample.


                All sampling lines shall be checked for the presence of
      condensation.     If condensation has occurred, adjustments In
      procedure shall be made either to prevent It or to compensate for
      the condense~ material In the sample ltne.


                Sufficient test data and notes shall be recorded at the
      time of the test by the personnel conducting the sampl Ing to permit
      the determination of emissions and to permit evaluation of
      results at a later date.


'-'             All sampling lines shall be leak-free.   The distance between
      the sample gas Inlet and the sample collection or analytical
      device shall be as short as feasible.     The sampling I Ines shall
      be composed of Inert materials.     The sampling lines shall be
      purged thoroughly with sample gas prior to the taking of the
      sample.


                Sampling equipment which comes In contact with the collected
      sample shall not alter the composition, and shalt be used In such
      a manner that significant leaks can be detected.


                1.3 Alternate Methods: EPA methods may be used alternately
      without prior approval.    Alternate methods may be approved at the
      discretion of the Chief of Source Test.     The burden of proof of


                                           5
equlvelency Is on the person seeking alternete status.     Such
elternate status may be granted on a "one-time only" basis.       Al I such
requests     and supporting documentation shall be made In writing.


       1.4 NSPS and NESHAPS Testing: The Bey Aree Air Qua I lty
Management District has been delegated authority by the U.S. Envfr­
onmentel Protection Agency (EPA) to enforce tbe New Source Perfor­
mance Stenderds (NSPS) and the National Emission Standerds for
Hazardous Air Pollutants CNESHAPS).


       EPA requires that ell testing for these standards use rts
methods unless alternete or equivalent status to It's methods has
been granted.    The District has been granted alternate status for
the lnstrumentel S02 and NOx procedures, ST-19A and ST-13A end
the lntegreted sulfer oxide procedure, ST-20.     EPA methods are
published In Title 40 Code of Federal Regulations, Part 60, end ere
evei Iable from the U.S. Government Printing Office.


       1.5 Tests Conducted by Other Persons: Any person who conducts
e source test for the purpose of furnishing data to the Afr Pol luting
Officer Is Subject to the following conditions:


       1.5.1 Notifications: The Chief of Source Test shall be
notified et least one week prior to the date the test Is to be
conducted.    Source Test Engineers from the District shall observe
any such tests as deemed necessary.


       1.5.2 Methodology: The procedures set forth In this volume
shall be adhered    to In any test except es provided In paragraph 1.3.

                                    6
              1.5.3 Data Presentation: Data shal I be collected and
'-"
      presented in the format of the forms shown herein.     Al I cal-
      culations shal I be Included and clearly Identified.


             1.5.4 Process Information: Where applicable, pertinent
      process data Including rates during the test and maximum capacities
      shall be Included In 'the data.   Confldentlal Information shall be
      clearly marked as such.


             1.5.5 Test Review: Tests furnished the District shall be
      sent to the attention of the Source Test Section for review and
      disposition.


             1.6 Confidentiality of Process Information:

      Process Information Is a necessary part of any source test In that
      the test data represents those process conditions at the time of
      the test.


      Source Test records are public Information.     However, proprietary
      Information Is not.     Information of a confidential nature should
      be clearly labeled to Insure proper treatment.


             1. 7 Appl lcabl I lty
      Each test procedure Is applicable to a specific regulation.
      The designation appears In the upper left hand corner of the
      procedure.

                           Source Test Procedure ST-1A
                          AMMONIA, CONTINUOUS SAMPLING


REF: Reg. 7-303
1. Appltcabtllty

       1.1 This method Is used to quantify emissions of ammonia.
It determines compliance with Regulation 7-303.
2. Prtnclple
       2.1 A gas sample Is extracted continuously from the sample
point and conditioned to remove water end particulate aatter.
A stalnless steel catalyst <et 750C) In a chemllumlnescent
analyzer converts ammonia (NH3) end nitrogen dlxotde (N0 2 ) to
the analyzer. By datennlntcg.the NO and N0 2 separately, the NH 3
concentration ts determined by difference.
3. Range end Sensitivity
       3.1 The minimum measurable concentration of NH3 Is 5%
of the total oxides of nitrogen.
           3.2 The minimum sensitivity of the analyzer shal I be !2%
of   f u I I s ca I e •

4.   1-nterfe ranees .
       4.1. Nitrogen bearing compounds Cother than NO and N02>
may cause tnterfeneces.
5. Apparatus
       5.1 Chemllumtnescent analyzer. Use a Thermo Electron Corp.
Model IOA analyzer equipped with a stainless steel catalyst
or Its equivalent.
       5 .2 Che r-t recorder. The recorder 110n I tors and records
the continuous output from the analyzer.
       5.3 Sample conditioning, zero air, and span gas system.
The assembly of this syste• ts shown In Fig. IY-1. The sample
conditioning system provides a dry, particulate free gas flow
to the Instrument. The zero air system provides clean dry
atmospheric air for Instrument calibration. The span gas
system provides a known concentration of nitric oxide for
use In caltbretlng the analyzer. Except as specified, all materials
which come In contact with either the sample or span gases must be
constructed of Teflon or stainless steel.



                                         8
'-,_.·
               5.4 Semple probe. Use e borosilicate glass tube of
         sufficient length to traverse the stack being tested.  If the
         stack temperature exceeds 425C C800F), use a quartz probe.
         Other probes ere acceptable subject to approval by the Source
         :rest Sect-Ion.
              5.5 Condensers. Use modified Greenberg-Smith lmplngers
         with the Impaction plates removed and the Inlet tube shortened
         to a length of 10cm (4 Inches), or equivalent.
              5.6 Cool Ing system.   laaerse the lmplngers In an Ice bath
         during the test.
              5.7 Particulate fitter. Use a Balston type 95 holder
         with a grade B fitter, or equivalent, In the sample system.
              5.8 Pumps. Use leak-free, Teflon-lined, diaphragm pump~
         In the sample end zero air systems. The pumps must have a
         capacity of at least 40 llters/mlnCl.5 CFM) to 28 liters/min
         C1.0 CFM).
               5.9 Back-pressure regulator. Use a beck-pressure regulator
         to maintain the sample and zero gas sample pressures to the
         Instrument et five pslg.
              5.10 Gas scrubber. Use a bed of silica gel, Ascarlte
         (or soda-lime), and charcoal to remove moisture, carbon
         dioxide, and hydrocarbons from the zero air system.
              5.11 Span gas.· Use a high-pressure cylinder containing a
         known concentration of NO In nitrogen. The span gas concentra­
         tion must be In the same range as the source being tested.
         6. Pre-Test Procedures
              6.1 Warm-up the ·1nst'ru111ent according To manufacturer's
         Instructions.
              6.2 Assemble the sampling system and analyzer as shown in
         Figs IV-I and IV-2.
              6.3 Leak-test the sampling system by st'artlng the pump,
         plugging the probe, end determining that' the pressure To the
         analyzer falls to zero. Other leak-tests ere acceptable
         subject to the approval of the Soa~ce Test Section.
               6.4 Introduce zero air, Into the analyzer and zero the
         Instrument according to manufacturer's Instructions.
              6.5 Introduce span gas Into the analyzer end calibrate
         the Instrument according to menufecturer's Instructions.
              6.6 Conduct a preliminary concentration Traverse (according
         to ST-18) to determine tf stratification of stack gases exists.
         If the NH3 concentration at any point differs from the average
                                     ., 9
concentration by more then tOJ, traverse the stack during
the test. If not, sample et any single point.
     6.7 Set-up the chart recorder according to manufacturer's
Instruct Ions.
7. Semp I Ing
     7.1 Each test run shell be of thirty minute duration
when testlng emissions from continuous operations. Each test
run et batch process operations whall be for 90% of the batch
time ~r thirty minutes, whichever Is lets.
     7.2 Introduce s~mple gas Into the analyzer at the seme
flow rate used to cellbrete the analyzer.
     7.3 Maintain Ice In the cool Ing system throughout the test.
     7.4 Cellbrate the analyzer before end after each test run.
Record each step of -the process clearly on the chert recording.
     7.5 Conduct three test runs.
8. Caleuletlons
     8.1 From the chert recording determine the time-averaged
concentration 6t NH3 one dry basis for each run.
9. Reporting
     9.1 Report the results Indicated on Figure IV-3.
     9.2 These values ere elso ••••• (see II.I of ST-24).

                           AMMONIA, INTEGRATED SAMPLING
REF: Reg. 7-303
1. Appllcablllty
       1.1 This method Is used to quantify emissions of ammonia.
It determines compliance with Reg. 7-303.
2. Prlnclple
       2.1 Sample gas Is drawn through a solution of 0.1 normal
CO.IN) hydrochlortc acid which absorbs the ammonla;.The ammonia
is then analyzed according to Analytical Prodecure lab-I.
3. Range
       · 3.1 The minimum measurable concentration of ammonia Is
   ppm et the sample volume specified In this procedure.
       3.2 Elevated concentrations of ammonia may be determined
by Increasing the concentration of the absorbing reagent,
hydrochlorlc acid solution. The concentration of reagent to
be used may be determined by stoichiometry, ellowlng e 50% excess.

           None Known.

    REF:Reg. 11-3-301

    2 • Pr I nc i p I e

    .oot gr/S0CF.

    type or equivalent.
            5.3 Connections.                    The connection between
    the fl lters and the first lmplnger must be able to withstand stack
    temperatures. Vinyl tubing Is acceptable In making all other
    connections.

    with a known coefflclenct which Is constant within *5i over the
    entire working range, The pltot tube coefficient Is determined
    by placing both the S-type and the standard pltot tube In a gas
    stream and measuring the pressure head with both over the entire
    velocity range of Interest. Calculate the coefficient of the
    Type-S pltot ~ube as follows:

       5.5 Temperature measuring device. Use a Chromel-Alumel

~                     CARBON DIOXIDE, CONTINUOUS SAMPLING

    REF:-· Reg. 6.310.l
    I • App I I cab I I f ty

         I.I Thfs method rs used to quantify emissfons of carbon
    dioxide.  It determines compliance with Reg. 6.310.1.
    2. Princiole
         2.1 A continuous representative gas sample is extracted
    from the sampllng point and condftloned to remove water and
    particulate material. The carbon dfoxlde concentration Is
    determined by passing a smal I portion of the sample through a
    non-dispersive Infrared analyzer (NOIR) tuned to a frequency
    at which carbon dioxide absorbs Infrared radiation.
    3. Range and Sensitivity

          3.1 The minimum and maximum measurable concentrations of
    CO 2 depend on the samp I e ce I I I ength In the ana I yzer.
            3.2 The minimum sensltlvlty of the MOIR analyzer shal I be
    '!:2:: ot full scale.
    4. Interferences
          4.1 Possible Interferences include me~hanol, ethanol and
    water.

    5. Aoparatus
         5.1 Carbon dioxide analyzer. Use an Infrared lndustrfes
    Inc., Model 703 analyzer or its equlvalent.


         5.2 Chart recorder. The recorder monitors and records
    the continuous output from the analyzer.
          5.3 Sample condftionfng, zero air, and span gas system.
    The assembly of this system rs shown fn Figure fV-JS. The sample
    conditioning system provfdes a dry, particulate free gas flow
    to the instrument. The zero air system provfdes clean dry
    atmospheric air for Instrument calibration. The span gas
    system provfdes a known concentration of co 2 for use fn
    cal lbratlng the analyzer. Except as specified, al I materials
    which come in contact wfth either the sample or span gases
    must be constructed of Teflon or stain less steel.
      5.4 S~mple probe. Use e borosll lcate glass tube of
sufficient length to traverse the stack being tested.  If
the stack temperature exceeds 425C (SOOr), use a quartz proDe.
Other probes ere acceptable subject to aooroval bv t~e Source
Test Section.                                    ·
     5.5 Condensers. Use modified Greenberg-Smith lmplngers
with the impaction plates removed and the inlet tube shortened
to a length of 10cm (4 Inches), or equivalent.
     5.6 Cooling syste~.   Immerse the impingers in en Ice bath
during the test.
    5.7 Particulete filter. Use a Balston type 95 holder
withe grade S filter, or equivalent, in the sample system.
        5.8 Pumps. Use leak-free, Teflon-lined, diaphragm pumps
in the sample and zero ~tr systems. The pumps must heve a
cap a·c i ty of at I east. 2 8 I I t •rs/ra f n ( f • O CF M) •
      5.9 Beck-pressure regulator. Us~ e beck-pressure regulator
to meintein the sample and zero ges sample pressures to the
instrument at five pslg.
     5.10 Gas scrubber. Use a bed of si lice gel, Ascarite
(or soda-I ime), end charcoal to remove moisture, cerbon dioxide,
end hydrocarbons from the zero air system.
     5.1 I Span ges. Use e hlgh-pres'sure cyl !nder conteining e       .........._
known concentration of CO 2 in elr or nitrogen. The span gas
concentration must be In the same range es t~e source being tested.
6. Fre-Test Procedures
     6.1 Warm-up the Instrument according to manufecturer's
instructions.
     6.2 Assemble the sampling system as shown in Figure .IV-15.
     6.3 Leak-test the sampl Ing system by starting the pump,
plugging the probe, and determining that the pressure to the
analyzer fal Is to zero. Other leek-tests are acceptable
subjecT to the approval of the Source Test Section.
      6.4 Introduce zero air, Into the analvzer and zero the
Instrument according to manufacturer's instructions.
     6.5 Introduce span gas into the analyzer end eat lbrate
the instrument according to manufacturer's instructions.
     6.6!Conduct e pref lmlnary concentration traverse ~ccording
 to ST-18) to determine If stratification of the stack gases exists.




                             27
If the CO concentration at any point differs from the average
concentraiion by more than 10~, traverse the stack durln~ the
test.  If not, sample at any single point.
     6.7 Set-up the chart recorder according to manufacturer's
Instructions.
7. Samp I I n_g

     7.1 Each test run shall be of thirty minute duration
when testing emissions from continuous operations. Each test
run at a batch process operation shal I be for 9O~ of the batch
time or thirty minutes. whichever Is less.
     7.2 Introduce sample gas Into the analyzer at the same
flow rate used to callbrate the analyzer.
      7.3 Maintain Ice in the cool Ing system throughout the test.
     7.4 Calibrate the analyzer before and after each test
run. Record each step of the process clearly on the chart
recording.
      7. 5 Conduct .three test runs.

   - . .

other poocedures end shell be reported with those test results.

                       Source Test Procedure ST-6
               CARBON MONOXIDE CONTINUOUS SAMPLING

REF: Reg. 8-1-110.3, 10-8-301.
I. Appl tcab i I tty
     I .I This method Is used to Quantify emissions of carbon
monoxide.   It determines compliance with Reg. 8-1-110.3.
2. Principle
     2.1 A continuous representative gas sample is extracted
from the sampling point end conditioned to remove water and
partlculate materiel. The carbon monoxide concentration ~s
determined by passing e smell portion of the sample through e
non-dispersive Infrared analyzer CNOIR) tuned to a frequency
et which carbon monoxide absorbs Infrared radiation.
3. R~nge_and Sensitivity
     3.1 The minimum and maximum measurable concentrations
of CO depend on the sample cell length In the analyzer.
+    3.2 The minimum sensitivity of the NOIR analyzer shal I be
-2% of full scale.
4. Interferences
   -       .

     4.1 Cyanogen and methyl azide ere possible interfering
species.
5. Apparatus
     5.1 Carbon monoxide analyzer. Use a Mine Safety Appl lance
Co. LIRA ~odel 202 a~alyzer or Its eotiivalent.


     5.2 Chart recorder. The recorder monitors end records
teh continuous output from the analyzer.
     5.3 Sample conditioning, zero air, and span gas system.
The assembly of this system ts shown In Figure IV-16. The sample
conditioning system provides a dry, particulate free gas flow
to the Instrument. The zero air system provides clean dry
atmospheric air for Instrument calibratton. The span gas system
provides a known concentration of CO for use in cal tbrating the
analyzer. Except as specified, el I materials which come In contact
with either the sample or span contacting surfaces must be
constructed of Teflon or stainless steel.

                       Source Test Procedure ST-7
                       NON-METHANE ORGANIC CARBON
REF:     8-2-301
         8-4-301
         8-4-302.303
1.     Appllcablllty
       1.1 This method Is used to quantify emissions of non­
methane organic carbon for determining compliance with Reg. 8-2-301,
8-4-302,303. This method Is also us•d to quantify emissions of
total oxldlzable carbon <organic carbon plus carbon aonoxlde ),
hereafter referred to as "TOC" for determining compliance of
afterburners.
       1.2 Use method A (below) when carbon dioxide constitutes
over 85%, on a molar basis, of the total carbon <organic plus
Inorganic, as C1 > In the sample; otherwise use Method B.
2.     Princlple
        2.1 A continuous sample of effluent Is conditioned to
remove water and particulate material. A smell portion of the
conditioned effluent Is continuously analyzed by either method
A or B.

        2.1.1. Method A. The Sample Is passed through a bed of
soda-I lme to remove carbon dioxide. and then through e combustion
tube to oxidize ell organic carbon and carbon monoxide to
carbon dioxide. The carbon dioxide concentration Is contlnuously
monitored ·with a non-dispersive Infrared analyzer (NOIR) to
Indicate the TOC concentration In the sample.
        2.1.2 Method· B. The sample Is divided Into two equal
volume streams, one of which passes through the combustion tube.
The streams are alternately analyzed ~Ith the NOIR for carbon
dio~lde. The difference In analyses between the streams
represents the total oxldlzable carbon (TOC) concentration
of the sample.
       2.2 The methane content of the sample Is determined either
(A) by fl If Ing an evacuated cylinder with sample for subsequent
gas chromatographic analysis or (8) by directing a portion of
the conditioned sample through a bed of activated carbon to
remove non-methane organics and then Into a flame Ionization
detector. Subtraction of the methane concentration from TOC
yields non-methane Toe.                              ·
       2.3 If organic carbon alone Is measured a separate
continuous analysis for carbon monoxide with en NOIR Is
subtracted from the TOC.



                                 32
        3.     Range and Sensitivity
·"-'.
                 3.1 The minimum measurable concentration of TOC 10 ppm
        i f th e a p p ro pr I ate ND I R c e I I I s u s e d •
               3.2 The maximum concentration of TOC for wh1ch this
        procedure ts applicable rs SJ when the epproprlate NOIR eel I
        and sample dilution are used.
                 3.3 Use of thrs procedure requires a molar concentration
        ratio .. of oxygen to TOC of 10.1 or greater. Sample dilution
        may be used to achieve the 10:1 ratio.
                 3.4   The sensitivity of the NOIR Is !2J of ful I scale.
        4;     Interferences
               4.1 Negative bias •ay occur due to reaction of highly
        reactive organics (e.g. aldehydes or acids) with Internal
        surfaces or If the condensation point of the sample ts above
        the condenser temperature.                     '
               4.2 Additional negative bias may occur In Method A due
        to absorption of alcohols, acrds and aldehydes by the carbon
        dioxide scrubbing medium.

1              4.3 High concentrations of carbon monoxide may preclude
        the use of this procedure for the measurement of only the organic
..._.   portion of the TOC.
        5. Apparatus
                 5.1 Carbon dloxrde analyzer. Use an Infrared Industries
        Inc.    Gas Aenlyzer Model 703, or Its equivalent.
               5.2 Carbon monoxide analyzer. Use an Mine Safety Appliance
        Co. LIRA Model 202, or Its equivalent.
               5.3 Flame Ionization detector Conly If needed for methane
        analysis). Use a Beckman Model 400, or equivalent, If necessary
        for methane analysts.
               S.4     Chart recorder.   Record the continuous output from the
        analyzer.
               S.S Sample conditioning, zero air, and span gas system.
        Assemble this system as shown In Figure IV-18. Sample conditioning
        system shal I provide a dry, particulate-free gas flow to the
        Instrument.    The zero-alr system shall provide clean dry
        atmospheric atr for Instrument caltbratlon. The span-gas system
        shall provide known concentrarons of the appropriate gas for
        use In calibrating the analyzers. Except as specified, ell
        materials which come In contact with either the sample or span
        gases must be constructed of Teflon or stainless steel.

                          Source Test Method ST-8
                             DIMETHYLSULFIDE
REF:     Reg. 7-303
1.     Appl icabi I lty
       1.1 This method Is used to determine emissions of
dlmethylsulflde (OMS). It ls applicable to the determination
of compliance with Reulatlon 7-303
2.     Principle
       2.1 A sample Is collected In a clean Tedlar bag.      The
semple Is analyzed for OMS by chromatography with flame
photometric detection.
3.     Renge and Sensitivity
          3.1 The minimum measurable concentration of OMS Is 15 ppb.
       3.2 Non-linear response by the flame photometric detector
occurs for OMS concentrations over 10ppm. However, appropriate
dilution of the sample wl II allow concentrations up to 50 ppm to
be analyzed •
4.
           .
       Interferences - none known
5.     Apperatus
       5.1 Sempling p~obe. Use a borosl llcate glass tube fitted
et the downstreem end with an appropriate tubing connector.
       5.2 Sample bag. Use a Tedlar bag equipped with a capeclty
of et leest 10 liters and equipped with two stainless steel valves.
       5.3 Sempllng pump. Use a leak-free Teflon-lined ~laphragm
pump, or equlvelent, cepable of et least 0.5CFM.
          S.4   Sample lines,   Use Teflon.
        5.5. Drier/deodorizer. Use three glass cartridges fitted
with bell joints. The first contatns Orlerlte <calcium chloride);
the others contain ectlvated carbon and shall be followed by a
Pyr-ex wool fl lter-.
6.     Pre-Test Procedures
       6.1  Before going to the test site, assemble the train as
shown In Figure IV-24. Pur-ge the entire tr-aln, Including the Tedlar
beg, untll the discharge Is clean. The Tedler- bag Is considered
cleen when the laborator-y analysis deter-mines the OMS concentr-etlon
to be undetectable.

I. App I lcebt I tty

end partlculate mercury.  It determines compliance with

2. Principle
      2.1 The semple streem Is drawn through a solutlon of
Iodine monochlortde which collects the mercury In the gas

ere expected, use parttculate fitters es prescribed In ST-15.

gre i ns/SOCF.
      3.2 Elevated concentrations of mercury mey be determined
by lncreeslng the concentretlon of the Iodine monochlortde In
the ebsorblng reagent. The concentretlon of reegent to be used
mey be tetermlned by stoichiometry, ellowlng e 50% excess.

                                       MERCAPTANS
     REF:     Reg.    7-303

     1.     App I lcabl I ity
            1.1.Thls method Is used to determine emissions of mercaptans.
     It Is applicable to the determination of compliance with Reg. 7-303
     2.     Principia
           2.1 A sample Is collected In a clean Tedlar bag. The sample
    Is analyzed for mercaptans by chromatography with fl••• photometric
    detection.
    3.      Range and Sensitivity
               3.1    The minimum •~asurable concentration of mercaptans is
    20 ppb.
            3.2 Non-linear response by the flame photometric detector
    occurs for mercaptans concentrations over 19ppm. However, appropriate
    di lutton of the sample wl II allow concentrations up·to:5p ppm to
    be anelyzad.         ·
    4. Interferences
               None Known
    5.      Apparatus
            5.1 Sampling probe. Use a borosilicate gli:iss tube fitted at
    _the downstream and with an appropriate tubing connector.
           5.2 Sampltng bag. Use a Tedlar bag with a capacity of at
    least 10 I lters and equipped with two stainless steel valves.
           5.3 Sampllng pump. Use a Tea~-free_Teflon~I la~d diaphragm
    pump, or equivalent, capable of at least 0.5 CFM.
           5.4 Sample lines.         Use Teflon because of Its non-reactive
    properties.
           5.5 Drier/deodorizer. Use three glass cartridges fitted
    with bal I Joints. The~ftrs; contains Drier-It• (calclum chloride);
    the others contain activated carbon and shal I be fol lowed by a
    Pyrex wool filter.
    6.    Pre-Test Procedures
           6-1 Before going to the test site, assemble the train as
    shown In Figure IV-37. Purge the entire train, Including the Tedlar
    bag, untll the discharge Is clean. The tedlar bag Is considered
-   clean when the lab determines the mercaptan concentration to be
    undectable.
                                          52
          6.2 Evacuate the Tedler ~eg.
       6.3 At the sempl Ing site, assemble the train es shown in
Figure I, leaving out the drier-deodorizer.
7. Semp I in g
          7.1 For stack sources, insert the probe into the steck.
       7.2 For embient semple, semple where the odor appears to
be tho strongest.
       7.3 Start the pump end purge the ges to be sampled through
the pump end beg for five minutes.
       7.4 Then close the outlet valve on the Tedlar beg end fll I
the oag over e period of not less than three minutes.
          7.5 Three begs fllled es In 7.3 end 7.4 shell constitute
e test.
                    •
6. Post-Test Procedures
       8.1 The bags must be analyzed for merceptens within four
hours of collection. Refer to Anelytlcel Procedure Leb-3.
9. Reporting
       9.1 The mercepten concentration of each bag is reported as
in Figure IY-38.

                                     COLLECTION OF ODOROUS SAMPLES
          REF:      Reg. 7-401
          1.     Applicability
                 1.1. This procedure Is used for the collection of odorous
          samples to be evaluated according to Regulation 7-401.
          2•     Pr I nc I p I e
                 2.1 Sample gas Is drawn Into a clean Tedlar bag at stack
          temperature. When condensation Is expected the sample Is diluted
          with clean dry air at stack temperature.  If condensation Is not
          expected the gas sampfa:ls drawn directly Into a Tedlar bag.
                  2.2 The sample probe uses critical orifices for both the
          di lutton air and the gas sample. The two are mixed at the same
          temperature to as~ure a constant di lutlon.
          3.     Range and Sensitivity - not appl !cable
          4.     Interferences
                 4.1 Particulate material wl I I plug the critical orifice
          In the di fut ion probe.
                 4.2 Sample dilution must be sufficient to prevent
          condensation In the bag or probe.
          5.     Apparatus
                 5.1 01 lutlon probe.          The di lutlon probe Is constructed as ·
          shown In Figure IV-39.
                 5.1.1. All surfaces which contact the sample shall be Teflon,
          borosilicate glass or type 316 stainless steel.
          t
                  5.1.2 The critical orifices shall be sized to provide a
          di lutlon sufficient to prevent condensation In the sample gas.
          The dilution shall be at least 10:1.
                 5.2 Pump. Use a Teflon-fined diaphragm puap or equivalent
          capable of at least 20 Inches Hg vacuum. The puNp shall be fitted
          with an Inlet vacuum gauge and an Inlet flow control valve.
                 5.3 Sample bag. A Tedlar bag with two stainless steel
          valves and a 10 titer capacity.
                 5.4 Orler/deordorlzer. Use three glass cartridges fitted

-   .,I
          with ball Joints. The first contains Orlerlte; the others contain
          activated carbon and shat I be followed by a Pyrex wool filter.
                 5.5
          be Teflon.
                             Sampling fines.   All sample contacting tubing she I I

measuring the total (di luted) and undl luted sample.
6.    Pre-Sampling Procedures
       6.1 Purge the sample pump and Tedlar beg with clean,
dry air fore minimum of 12 hours prior to sample collection.
        6.2 Use the pump to be used for the test and
fll I the Tedler sample bag with clean dry air and close both
valves.
       6.2.1  Evaluate the beg according to Section 7-400 of
Regulation 7 within three hours prior to testJng.
         6.2.2 Two or 110re positive responses will dlsqual lfy the
beg   from being used.
         6.3     Assemble the train es shown In Figure IV-40.
       6.4 Start the pump, block the sample and air Inlets
end check that the flow out of the pump fells to zero.
       6.5 Start the pump end purge the stack gas through
the pump for et least three minutes.
         6.6     Ollutlon measurement.
       6.6.1 This step may be performed simultaneously with 6.5

       6.6.2 Start the pump end adJust the Inlet vacuum to et

       6.6.3 Block the air line end adJust the pump vacuum to

        7.3    Close the beg outlet and ti II the beg.
