                              Source Test Procedure ST-13A
                  OXIDES OF NITROGEN, CONTINUOUS SAMPLING
    REF:     aegs. 9-3-301
                        9-3-302
                       10-1-301

    I • Ap p I i c a b I I I t_y
         I.I This method Is used to quantity emlssfons of oxides
    of nitrogen.  It determines compliance with Regs.·9-3-301,
    9-3-302 and 10-1-301.
    2. Principle
          2.1 A gas sample Is extracted continuously from the sampl in~
    point and conditioned to remove water and particulate matter.
    Nftrlc oxide (NO) emisslons'ere determined by passing I smal I·
    portion of the sample through a chemllumlnescent analvzer. The
    chemiluminescent processes Is based on the I lght given oft when
    nitric oxide and ozone react. Nltrog~n dioxide (~0 2 > concentrations
    era determined by passing the sample through a catalyst which
    reduces the N02 to NO. The total oxides ~f nitrogen concentr~tion
    (N0 2 + NO) Is then determined by chemiluminescence.
    3. Rang_e__~_nd Sens]tlvft:Y

         3. I The minimum and maximum measurable concentrations


-   of NOx depends on the specific chemiluminescent analyzer.
         3.2 The minimum sensitivJty of the analyzer shal I be
    i2% ot tul I scale.
    4. I nte rterences
            4. I   If the molybdenum catalyst Is used, ·compounds containing
    nltl"ogen       Cother than ammonia) may cause interference.
    5. Apparatus
         5.1 Oxides of nitrogen analyzer. Use a Thermo Electron
    Corp. Model IOA zinzilyzer or Its equivalent.
         5.2 Chart recorder. The recorder monitors zind records
    the contfnuous output fl"om the analyzer.
          5.3 Sample condttfonlng,· zero afr, and span gas system.
    The assembly of thf~ system Is shown In Figure.fV-42. The ~ample
    condftfonlng system provides a dry, particulate free gas flow
    to the Instrument. The zero air system provides clean dry
    atmospheric air for fnstrument callbratlon. The span gas
    system provides a known concentration of NO tor use· In calibrating
    the analyzer. Except as specified, al I materials whfch come In
    contact with either the sample or span gas must be constructed
    of Teflon or stainless steel.



                                            57
           5.4 Sample probe. Use a borosll lcate glass tube of
      sufficient length to traverse the stack being tested.  If the
      stack temperature exceeds 425C (800F), use a quartz probe. Other
      probes are acceptable subject to prfor approval by the Source
      Test Section.
           5.5 Condensers. Use modified Greenberg-smith lmpln~ers
      with the Impaction plates reMoved and the Inlet tube shoftened
      to a length of 10cm (4 Inches>, or equivalent.
           5.6 Cool Ing system.   Immerse the lmpingers In an tee baTh
      during the test.
           5.7 Parttculate filter. Use a Baisten type 95 holder
      with a grade B filter, or equivalent, In the sample system.
                                                                              -
           5.8 Pumps. Use l~ak-free, Teflon -I fned, diaphragm pumps
      In the sample and zero air systems. The pumps must have a
      capacity of at least 28 liters/min (1.0 CFM).

           5.9 Back-pressure regulator. Use a back-pressure regulator
     to maintain the sample and zero gas sample pressures to the
     Instrument at five pslg.
          5.10 Gas scrubber. Use a bed of sll lea gel, Ascarite
     (or soda-I Imel, and charcoal to remove moisture, carbon dioxide,
     and hydrocarbons from the zero air system.


·-        5.11 Span gas. Use a hl~h-pressure cylinder contafnfng a
     known concentration of NO in Nitrogen. The span gas concentration
     must be In the same range as the source being tested.
     6. Pre-Test Procedures
          6.1 Warm-up the Instrument accordln~ to manufacturer's
     Instructions.
          6.2 Assemble the sampl Ing system and analyzer as shown I~ Fig~.
     I Y-4~ end• tY-43.
           6.3 Leak-test the sampling system by starting the pump,
     plugging the probe, end determinlng that the pressure to the
     analyzer fal Is to zero. Other leak-tests are acceptable subject
     to the approval of the Source Test Section.
           6.4 Introduce zero err, lnto the analyzer and zero the
     Instrument according to manufacturer's Instructions.

                                 Source Test Procedure ST-138
                            OXIDES OF NITROGEN, INTERATEO SAMPLE
    REF: REGS.        9-3-301       9-3~304     10-1-301
                      9-3-302       9-4-301
                      9-3-303       9-4-302
    1.   Appl lcabl I lty
            1.1 This method Is used to quantify emissions of oxides
    of nitrogen.  It determines compliance with Regs. 9-3-301, 9-3-302 ,
    9-3-303, 9-3-304, 9-4-301, 9-4-302, 10-1-301 and Is an acceptable
    alternate method to ST-13A.
    2.   Principle
           2.1 Sample gas Is drawn through an alkallne permanganate
    which absorbs the oxides of nitrogen. The oxides of nitrogen
    are subsequently analyzed for nitrite (N02> as specified In
    Analytical Procedure La~-7A.
    3.   Range and Sensitivity
           3.1 The minimum measurable concentration of oxides of
    nitrogen is 6 ppm at tne sample vclume specified in this
    procedure.
           3.2 Tbe maximum concentration for which this procedure
    hes been proven Is 1800 ppm.


-   4.   lnterterence
              None.
    5 •. Apparatus
              5.1     Probe.    The probe Is constructed of borosilicate glass
    tubing.
           5.2 Absorbers. Use three Greenberg-Smith lmplngers
    modified by replacing the orifice and Impaction plat9s ~Ith
    semi-fine frits of approximately 70 por.oslty. The thlrd lmptnger
    has a thermometer attached to the Inlet.
           5.3        Cool Ing system.   An Ice bath Is used to contain the
    lmptngers.
           5.4 Sample pump. Use a leak-free vacuum pump capable of
    maintaining a 0.5 CFM flow rate at 15 Inches of mercury-vacuum.
    The pump has a flow control valve and vacuum gauge attached to
    the Inlet.
           5.5. Silica gel tube. Approximately 500cc of silica gel
    with Drierite Indicator are used to Insure that the gas entering
    the dry gas meter is dry.
           5.6 Dry test meter. Use a dry gas test meter accurate

-   within !2% of the true volume and equipped with a thermometer
    to measure the outlet temperature.

      I • Apo I i cab I I i ty
           I.I This method Is used to quantify emissions of oxygen.
      It determines comp I lance with Regs. 9-3-301 and 9-3-302.
     2. Principle
          2.1 A continuous representative gas sample Is extracted
     from the sampling point and conditioned to re~ove water end
     particulate material. A smal I portion of the smaple is passed
     through a fuel eel I type oxygen analyzer which measures the
     olectrlcal current generated by the oxidation reaction at the
     gas/fuel eel I inter-face.

     3. Ran~• and S~nsl!!vity
             3.1 The minimum measurable concentration of oxygen Is 0.1%.

             3.2 The upper I Jmit depends on the range of the instrument.
           3.3 The sensitivity of the          o~. enalvzer shal I be ±2~ of
     ful I scale •
     . 4. Interferences
          4.1 Halogens and halogenated comoounds wi 11 cause a positive
     interference. Acid gases wi 11 consume the fuel eel I and
     cause a slow calibration drift.

     5. Apparatus
          5.1 Oxygen analyzer. Use a Teledyne Analytical              Instruments
     Inc., Model 326 analyzer or its equivalent.
          5.2 Chert recorder. The recorder monitors end records
     the continuous output from the analyzer.

     The assembly of this system ts shown In Fig.· 1v~.-a. The sample
     conditioning system provides e dry, partlculaTe free gas flow
     to the Instrument. The zero air system provides clean dry
     atmospheric air for Instrument eel ibretlon. Except as specified,
     el I materials which come In contact with either the sampl~ or
     span should be constructed of Teflon or stalnless steel.

     5. I I Span gas. The analyzer is spanned using dry air fron

                                          PARTICULATE
    REF:      Regs.      6-310     10-1-301

    1•     App I i cab i I I ty

           1.1 This method ls used to quantify particulate emissions.
    It determines compliance with Reg~lations 6-310, 6-311 and 10-1-301.
    2.     Principle
            2.1 Stack gases are drawn lsoklnetlcal ly through glass
    fiber fl lters at stack conditions. The weight of particulate
    matter,    Including any material that condenses at or above the
    stack temperature, Is determined gravimetrical ly after the
    remova I of uncombed ·water.
    3.     Range
           3.1 The minimum particulate emissions measurable by this
    procedure Is .001 gr/SOCF.
    4.     Interferences


-          4.1 Sul fer oxides, sulfates and some other species which
    are not particulate at stack conditions may react with the fl lter
    medium so as to produce a spurious weight gain. This phenoGona
    is minimized by use of a back-up filter as a blank.
    5.     Apparatus

    to the nozzle shown In Fig IV-49. The probe nozzle shall be
    constructed of borosll icate glass, quartz, or stainless steel.

    wl II contain threefl lter tubes and the probe nozzle In a leak-free
    manner. Fig IV-50 shows an acceptable fl lter tube holder configuration.

    equivalent.         Corning Glass Works Glass, Catalogue 13950 Is
    acceptable.

      I. Apol lcabi I tty

             I .I This mP.thod is used to quantifv emissions of ohenol ic
      compounds as phenol.      It Is appl I cable to the determination of
      comp I iance with Regulatlon.7-303.
      2. Prlncicle

      CO. IN) sodium hydroxide which a~sorbs tho phenolic compounds.
      The phenol le compounds are then analyzed by qas chromato~ra~hv
      as specified in Analytical Procedure Lab-8.
      3. Ranqc and Sensitivity

      procedure.

      excess.

              STACK GAS VELOCITY A:rn V'JLU 1 1:T~IC FLOW ~~-TE

     I. Applicebillty
          I. I This procedure is used to determine stack gas velocitv
     end to quantify volumetric flow rate.    It is applicable for use
     In determining compliance with any re9ulation of the BAAQMO
     ~herein stack gas velocity or flow rate is required.
     2. Principle
          2.1 ihe stack gas velocity is detar~ined bv usin~ a ~ito~
     tube to ~eesure the velocity heads at ore-doterminec treversn
     points CST-18) across the stack. The stack gas temocrature,
     static pres~ura, end molecular weight and moisture content ar~
     also measured. The volumetric flow rate can then be deterMined
     from knowledge of the duct geometry. Velocity measurenents
     should be made from e Type "A" sempl ing point whenever ~ossihlc.
     If sampling fecfl itles permit, velocity traverses are conduct~c
     from two ports which ere in radii of 90° •

                        STACK TRAVERSE POINT DETERMINATION

I • Ao p I I cab I I I ty
       I.I This procedure Is used to determine the number and
l?catlon of traverse points required when determining stack gas
velocity or extracting a sample from a tvpe "P.'' sample point.
2. Principle
      2.1 Depending on the stack geometry. a cross-section of the stack
perpendicular to the gas flow Is divided Into a specified number
of equal areas. Each traverse point Is then located et pre-
determined points In each erea.
3. Celculetfons
      3.1 For circular ducts, the following guide! Ines for the
number of traverse points shal I be fol lowed:

used to deter-mine the locatlon of each tr-averse point:

used   to determine the equivalent diameter of the stack:
                equlvelent dfameter                     •       2Clength)Cwldth)

      The number of traverse points is then determined from Table 18-1

    REF:     Regs. 9-1-302             10-1-301

    1.     Applicability
           1.1 This method ts used to quantify emissions of
    sulfur dioxide (S0 2 >. It determines compliance with
    Regulations 9-1-302, 9-1-304 thru 310 and 10-1-301.
           1.2 The EPA has granted alternate status to EPA
    Method 6 for this method.  It determines compliance
    with sulfur dioxide emissions covered under Regulation 7
    <New Source Performance Standards).
    2.     Principia
         · 2.1 A continuous representative gas sample Is
    extracted from the sampl Ing point and conditioned to remove
    water and particulate material. The sulfur dioxide concentration
    Is determined by passing a small portion of the sample through
    an ultraviolet analyzer.
    3.     Range and Sensitivity
              3.1   The minimum and maxlm~m measurable concentrations
(
    of   so 2 depends on the samp I e ca 11Iength In the ana Iyzer.
           3.2 The minimum sensitivity of the UV anlayzer shall be

    4. Interferences

    disulfide wi 11 cause Interferences.
    5.   Ap pa rat us

    Nemours and Co. Model 400 photometric analyzer or Its
    equivalent.
           5.2 Chart recorder. The recorder monitors end records
    the continuous output from the analyzer.
           5.3 Sample conditioning, zero air, end span gas system.
    The assembly of this system Is shown In Fig. IV-60. The sample
    conditioning system provides• dry, particulate free gas t•ow
    to the Instrument. The zero err system provides clean dry

                          Source Test Procedure   ST-198
                   TOTAL SULFUR OXIDES INTEGRATED SAMPLE
REF:     Regs. 9-1-302          10-1-301
               9-1-304 thru 310
1.     Appllcabi I tty
       1.1 This method is used to quantify emissions of total
sulfur oxides. It determines compl lance with Regulation 9-1-302,
9-1-304 thru 310 and 10-1-301, It Is an acceptable alternate
method to ST-19A.
2. Principia
       2.1 Sample gas Is drawn through 3% hydrogen peroxide
solution which absorbs the sulfur oxides. The peroxide solution
Is then analyzed according to Analytical Procedure,Lab-11.
3.     Range
       3.1 The Minimum measurable concentration of sulfur
oxides Is 7ppm at the sample voluffle specified In this procedure.
       3.2      The maximum measurable· concentration of sulfur oxides
Is 2.5j.
4.     Interference
       4.1 Tne presence of strong acids, such as hydrochloric
acid or sodium bl~ulflte give erroneously high results. The
presence of alkaline gases or reactive basic solids, such as
ammonia or calclum oxide give erroneously low results.
5.     Apparatus
       5.1 Probe.        The probe Is constructed of borosll icate
glass tubing.
       5.2 A~sorbers. · Use three Greenberg-Smith lmplngers.
The final lmplnger has a thermo•etar attached to the Inlet stem.
       5.3 FIiter. Use a glass filter tube of the same type
as Is used In ST-15.
       5.4     Cool Ing system.   An tea bath Is used to contain the
tmplngers.
       5.5 Sample pump. Use a leak-free vacuum pump capable of
maintaining a 0.5 CFM flow rate at 15 Inches of mercury. The
pump must have a flow control valve and vacuum gauge attached
to the Inlet.




                                      86
       5.6 SI Ilea gel tube. Use approxrmately 500cc of silica gel
(with a 0rlerlte Indicator> to Insure that the gas entering the         --.
dry test meter Is dry.
       5.7 Dry test meter. Use a dry test meter accurate
within !2% of the true volume and equipped with a thermometer
to measure the outlet temperature.
       5.8 Connections. Use Teflon tubing In making all conn­
ections that come In contact with the sample. Vinyl tubing Is
acceptable for ell other connections.
       5.9 Barometer. Use a be~ometer the~ Is accurate to
within !0.2 Inches of aercury.
       5.10 Rotameter.       Use a c~llbrated rctameter to measure
the sampllng rate.
6.   Reagents
         6.1 Hydrogen peroxide solution, 3%. Prepare a solutlon
of   3%, by volume, hydrogen peroxide In dlstl lled water.
7.   Pre-Test Procedures
        7.1     Add 100 ml of 5S!ii292 t9-each of:two~tmptngers.
        7.2     Stopper the lmplngers.
       7.3 Retain 100 ml of the hydrogen peroxide solution to
analyze as a blank.
        7.4     Assemble the sa~pllng train es shown In Figure IV-62.
       7.5 Leak-test the sampling train by starting the pump,
plugging the probe, and adjusting the pump Inlet vacuum to 10
Inches Hg. The leak rate must not exceed 0.02 CFM through the
dry test meter. Before. stopping the pump, carefully release
the plug In the sample probe to avoid backflow of the lmplnger
solutlon.
      7. 6 Record the In It I e I dry test meter read I ng.·add ••
barometric pressure as shown In Figure IV-6j.
8.   Sempllng
       8.1 Each test run shell be of thirty minute du rat Ion
when testing emissions from continuous operations. Each test run
at batch process operations shell be for 90% of. the batch time or
thirty minutes, whichever Is less.
       8.2      Pos]tfon the probe at the sampling point and start
the pump.
       8.3 Sample at a constant rate of 14.3 I lter/mln C0.5 CFM>
during the test es determined by the rotemeter. Use the rotameter
only to esteblfsh the Initial sampling rate. Then remove It from
the system.

           8.6 At the concl~slon of each run, stop the pump, remove
     the probe from the stack, and record the final meter reeding.
            8.7 Take three consecutive samples.
     9. Post-Test Procedures
           9.1 Immediately after each test run, stopper _the lmplngers
     to minimize sample losses.
           9.2 lndlvfduelly anelyze the hydrogen peroxide solutfons
     for total sulfur oxide content according to analytlcal procedure
     Lab II.

                      SOURCE TEST PROCEDURE ST-20
     SULFUR DIOXIDE, SULFUR TRIOXIDE, SULFURIC ACID MIST

1. Appl lcabl I lty
     I.I This aethod Is used to quantify emissions of sulfur­
dioxide, sulfur- trioxide and sulfur-le acid mist. It determines
compllanee with Regulations 6-320 and 6-330 for SULFUR TRlnXIDE

end 12-61301 for SULFUR DIOXIDE.
     1.2 This method, modified withe glass fiber disc filter
as the back-up S03 filter, has been given alternate status by
the EPA to EPA Method e. It may be used to determine compliance
with oxides of sulfur regulations under Reguletlon 9.
2. Pr-lnclple
     2.1 Sulfuric acid mist, sulfur trioxide and sulfur dioxide
are collected In a slngle extractive sampllng train. Acid mist
Is trapped In a quartz wool plug In the sample probe end Is
subsequentfy,analyzed with en acid-base titration. Sulfur
trioxide Is ebsor-bed In an soi lsopropyl alcohol (IPA)/weter­
solutlon with a quer-tz wool beck-up fllter end Is analyzed
using enalytlcal procedure LAB 12. Sulfur dioxide Is ebsor-bed
                                                                      -
In a ~queous hydrogen peroxide solutlon and Is analyzed using
enalytlcal p~ocedur-e LAB 12.
3. Range

      3.1 The minimum measurable concentrations using this method
I lsted below are:
                     3.1.1 Acid mist - .0002 gr-/tt3 as H2so
                     3.1.2 Sulfur trioxide - 7ppm            4
                     3.1.3 Sulfur dioxide - 7ppm
     3.2 The maximum measurable concentrations using this method
llsted below are:
                     3.2.1 Acid mist - undetermined
                     3.2.2 Sulfur trioxide - 350ppm
                     3.2.3 Sulfur dioxide - 2.5J
4.   Interferences
     4.1 Acid mist and sulfur dioxide.   The presence of strong
acids or salts such as hydrochlorlc acid or- sodium blsulflte
give erroneously high results. The presence of alkallne gases
or reactive basic sol Ids such as ammonia or calcium oxide give
erroneously low results.


                                 90
     4.2 Sulfur Trioxide. Sulfur dioxide at 2000 ppm or over
may oxidize to sulfur tr1oxlde In the IPA/H 2 o solutlon. For
this reason, two IPA/H20 tmplngers are used In series. The
sample concentration of S03 as Indicated by the second fmpfnger
Is regarded as a blank analysts and Is subtracted from the
analysts of the first lmptnger.
5. Apparatus
     5.1 Probe. Use a borosf lfcate glass ~robe long enough to
extend across the stack diameter.   If stack temperatures
exceed 8000F, use a quartz probe. The probe entrance shall·
be enlarg~d to approximately 20mm 1.0. for a length of 10cm.
The downstream end should be fitted with$ 900 elbow and a
ball Joint. The probe shall be heat-traced and Insulated ff
necessary to prevent sample condensation.
     5.2 Acid •1st ftlter. Insert approximately 3 grams of
quartz wool In the probe entrance.        ·
     5.3 Absorbers. Use four Greenberg-Smith lmpfngers, one
with the Inlet fitted with a ball Joint to receive the probe.
     5.4 FIiter. Approximately 10 grams of quartz or quartz wool
are packed Into a glass holder of the same specifications as
the ftlter holders used In ST-15.
     5.5 Cool Ing system.   An tee bath Is used to contain the
fmpfngers.
     5.6 Sample pump. Use a leak-free vacuum pump capable of
maintaining a 0.5 CFM flow rate at 15 Inches of mercury-vacuum.
The pump has a flow control valve and vacuum gauge attached to
the Inlet.
     5.7 SIiica gel tube. Use approximately 500cc of slllca gel
(with a Orferlte Indicator) to Insure that the gas entering
the dry test meter Is dry.
     5.8 Ory test meter. Use a dry gas test meter accurate
within t2j of the true volume and equipped with a thermometer
to measure the outlet temperature.
     5.9 Connections. Use Teflon tubing In making all connections
that come In contact with the sample. Vinyl tubing Is acceptable
for al I other connections.
     5.10 Barometer. Use a barometer that Is accurate to within
±0.2 Inches of mercury.
     5. II Rotameter.   Use a callbrated rotameter to measure
the sampling rate.

      8.5 Check the sample probe perlodlcally for condensate.

9. Post-Test Procedures
      9.1 Immediately after sampling, purge the lmplnger train with
ten cubic feet of S02-free ambient air at a rate of 0.5 CFM.
      9.2 Keep the two IPA solutions on Ice and stoppered untll
they are analyzed,
      9.3 Analyze the IPA solution within 24 hours after sampllng.

      10.1 Stack veloclty. Determine the stack gas velocity after

      10.2 Moisture content. Determine the moisture In the stack
gases In accordance with ST-23.
      10.3 Production rate. Determine the sulfuric acid or sulfur
production from plant data based on a 24 hour, daily production rate
In tons per day, If the test ts being conducted on a sulfur recovery
acid plant.
      10.4 Oxygen concentration.   Use ST-14 to determine the oxygen
concentration of the stack gas.

                                         Source Test Procedure ST-21


-         REF: Regs. ·12-1-301 thru 303
                                              TOTAL REDUCED SULFUR



          I. Appl icabl I lty

               I.I This method Is used to Quantify emissions of total reduced (T~S
        compounds as H2S, In the presence of sulfur dioxide. It
          determines compliance wJth RegulatJons 12-301~ 12-302 end
          12-303 •.

         2. Prlnclole

               2.1 A continuous representative pas sample Js extracted
         from the sampl Ing point and conditioned to remove water and
         particulate materlal. A small portion of the conditioned sample
         Is passed through a potassium cftrate/cltrlc ecJd buffer
         solutJon to remove sulfur dioxide and then through a combustfon
         tube to oxidize al I reduced sulfur to sulfur dioxide. The
         sulfur dioxide Is measured with an ultravlolet analyzer.
         3•   _Ra n g e _and_ Sen s ! t I v I t y
              3.1 The minimum measurable concentration of reduced
    I
         sulfur, as H2 S, Is 5 ppm with this method.

-                3.2 The maximum concentration of reduced sulfur is
         5000 ppm.

              3.3 The method has been proven in the presence of u~ to
         3500 ppm sulfur dioxide.
                 3.4 The analyzer sensltlvlty Is+ 2~ of Instrument scale.
         4. Interferences
                 4.1 None are known.
         5. Appara1:u~
              5.1                  Analyzer. Use en E.I. DuPont de
         Nemours Photometric analyzer Model 400 &Quipped with a stainless
         steel sample cell or Its equlvelent to determine so 2 concentrations.
              5.2 Chart recorder. The recorder monitors and records
         the continuous output from the analyzer.
               5.3 Sample conditioning, zero air, end span gas system.
         The assembly of this system Is shown In Fig. IV-68. The sample
         conditioning system provides a dry, particulate free ~es flow
         to the Instrument. The zero air system provides clean dry
         atmospheric air for Instrument eel lbretlon. The span gas

-        system provides a known concentration of soi for use In
         eel lbratlng the analyzer. Except es specified, all materials

                                                    96
whfch come In contact·wlth either the sample or span gases
                                                                   -
must be constructed of Teflon or stafnless steel.
     5.4 Sample probe. Use a boroslflcate glass tube long
enough to traverse the stack being tested. If the stack
temperature exceeds 425C {SOOF>, use e quartz probe. Other
probes are acceptable subJect to prJor approval by the Source
Test Section.
     5.5 Condensers. Use modified Greenberg-Smith fmplngers
with the fmpactton plates removed and the Inlet tube shortened
to a length of 10cm (4 Inches), or equlvefent.
     5.6 Cool Ing system.   Immerse the lmpfngers In an Ice
bath during the test.
     5.7 Pertfculate f'.llter. Use a Bafston type 95 holder
with a ~rade B fitter, or equivalent, In the sample system.
      5.8 Pumps. Use leek-free, Teflon-lfned, diaphragm pumps
In the sample and zero air system. The pumps mast have a
free-flow capacity of et least 40 titers/min (1.5 CFM).
      5.9 Back-pressure regufat~~. Use a beck-pressure regulator
to maintain the sample and zero-gas sample pressures to-the
Instrument et five pslg.                                           -
     5.10 Gas scrubber. Use a bed of jf llce gel, Ascerfte
(or sode-lfme), end charcoal to remove moisture, carbon
dioxide, and hydrocarbons from the zero-air system.
     5.11 Span gas. Use high-pressure cylinder containing a
known concentration of so 2 In nitrogen. The span gas con­
centration must be In the same range as the source being tested.
     5.12 Combustor. Use a system to oxidize elf reduced
sulfur compounds In the sample stream to so 2 • An example
of en acceptable combustion tube found adequate by the
BAAQMO Is shown fn Figures IV-69 and 1v~10.
     5.13 Sulfur dioxide scrubber.   Use three midget lmpfngers
containing the scrubbing reagent.
     5.14 FIiter. Use a Belston type 95 holder with a grade B
fllter following the combustion tube to reaove S03 end
particulate from the effluent.
6. Reagents
     6.1 Potassium Citrate/Citric Acid Buffer. Add 300 grams
of potassium citrate and 41.0 grams of citric acid to one
lfter of dlstlffed water. Check the ph of the solution end
adjust as necessary to 5.6 with the appropriate reagents.

    7. Pre-Test Procedures
         7.1 Warm-up the Instrument according to manufacturer's
    instructions.
            7.2 Assemble the sampling system as shown In Figure IV-71.
         7.3 Leak-test the sampling systeM by startln9 the pumn,
    plugging the probe, and checking that the pressure to the
    analyzer fal Is to zero.
          7.4 Introduce zero-air, Into the analyzer and zero the
    instrument according to manufacturer's instructions.
         7.5 Introduce span gas Into the analyzer and calibrate
    the Instrument acco~ding to manufacturer's instructions.
         7.6 Conduct a prellmlnary concentration traverse (in
    accordance with ST-18) to determine If stratification of the
    stack gases exists.  If the concentration of reduced sulfur
    compounds at any point differs from the average concentration
    by more then 10%, traverse the stack durln~ the test.  If not,
    sample at any single point.
         7.7 Prepare the chart recorder according to manufacturer's
    instructions.
-        7.8 Add approximately 25ml of the cltr~te buffer reagent
    to each of two midget impingers.
         7.9 Check the scrubber efficiency when the stack SO?
    concentration exceeds 300 ppm. Pass so 2 span gas In the rar.~~
    expected through the samole system at! flow rate
    of 2 CFH. Break-through should not occur In less than 20
    minutes.   If breakthrough occurs, add 25 ml more of the citrate
    ouffer roagent.
    8.   Samp I Ing
          8.1 Each test run shat I be of thirty minute duration when
    testing emissions from continuous operations. Each test run
    at a batch operation shal I be for 90:: of the batch time or
    thirty minutes, whichever Is less.
         8.2 Introduce sample gas Into the analyzer at the same
    flow rate used to callbrete the analyzer.
           8.3 ~:alntain ice In the cool Ing system throughout the test.
         3.4 Calibrate the analyzer before end after each test run.
    Record each step of the process clearly on the chart recordino.


-          8.5 Conduct throe test runs.
         8.6 Periodically pass so 2 span gas through the scrubber and
    ascertain that the analyzer reads zero. If it doesn't,replace the
    buffer ·reagent.

                                      TRIMETHYLAMINE


         I. Appl lcabl I lty
              I.I This method Is used to quantify emissions of
         Trfmethylamlng (TMA). It determines compllence with Reg.
         7-3-3.
         2. Prfnclple

    I . A_p p I i cab i I i ty

           I .I This method is used to determine the carbon monoxide,
    carjon dioxide and oxygen cont~nts of stack gases.   It is an
    acceptacle alternate to ST-5, ST-6, and ST-14.

    2. ~rinciple
           2.1 Stack gases are drawn at a constant rate into an evacuatec
    stainless steel Tank.  A desiccant is used to dry the gases before
    entering the tank.  After testing, the tank is positivelv oressurized
    wiT~ a known amount of drv nitrog~n.  The tank contents are then
    analyzed with an Orsat analyzer, and the results are corrected
    ~ac~ to pre-dilution conditions.

           PRESSURE DROP, PHASE II GASOLINE VAPOR RECOVERY

                    I
REF:    Reg. 1-7-600


1. Applicability
       l.l This method is used to deternine the pressure drop in
the vapor lines leading from the dispensinQ nozzle to the
t~derground tank, inclusively.       It is applicable in all cases
where a Phase II vapor balance system is used.


2. Principle
       2.1 The pressure drop during refuelin~ is simulated by
passing nitrogen through the Phase II recovery system at a
constant rate.     The resultant pressure drop is r,easured usin~
a pressure gauge.


3. ~nge
       3.1 The minimum and maximum pressure drops that can be
measured are dependent upon available pressure gauges.      Recommended
gauge ranges are 0-2 in. H2O and 0-10 in. H2O.


4. Interferences
   . .   .

       4.1 Any leaks in the nozzle vapor path, vapor hose, or
underground vapor return piping will result in a low pressure
drop measurement.

                HYDROGEN SULFIDE, INTEGRATED SAMPLING
REF:     Reg. 10-8-393
I. Appllceblllty
     I.I This method Is eppllceble for the determination of
hydrogen sulfide CH2S) emissions from stationery sources for
compliance with Reguletlon.10-8-303.
2. Principle
     2.t Hydrogen sulfide Is collected In e series of tmplngers
end reacted with elkellne cadmium hydroxide (CdCOH>2> to form
cadmium sulfide CCdS). The precipitated CdS Is dissolved In
hydrochloric acid end absorbed In en Iodine solution. The
iodine consumed Is related to the H2S content of the ges. The
sample train Is preced•d by en lmplnger containing hydrogen
peroxide to remove so 2 which Interfere with the determination.
3. Range and·Sensltlvlty
     3.1 The minimum measurable concentration of hydrogen sulfide
Is 0.0002 grelns/SDCF
4. Interferences                                                     -
     4.1 High concentrations of so 2 or other acid gases could
saturate the peroxide lmplnger end Interfere.
5. Apparatus
     5.1 Probe.    The probe Is constructed of borosll lcete glass
tubing.
        5.2 Absorbers. Use five Greenberg-Smith lmplngers. The
flnel    lmpinger hes e thermometer etteche~ to the Inlet stem.
     5.3 Cool Ing system.   An Ice beth Is used to contain the
lmplngers.
     5.4 Semple pump. Use e leek free vacuum pump capable of
maintaining e 0.5 CFM flow rate et 15 Inches of mercury. The
pump hes e flow control end vacuum gauge attached to the Inlet.
     5.5 Slllce gel tube. Use epproxlmately 500 cc of slllce
gel with a drlerlte Indicator to Insure dry gas entering the
dry gas meter.

         5.6 Ory test meter. Use a dry test meter accurate within
-   ±2% of the true volume and equipped with a thermometer to
    measure the outlet temperature.
         ,.1 Connections. Use Teflon tubing ln·maklng all connections
    that come In contact with the sample. Vlnyl tubing Is acceptable
    for all other connections.
         5.8 Barometer.     Use a barometer that ls accurate within ±0.2
    Inches of mercury.
         ,.9 Rotameter.     Use a calibrated rotameter to measure the
    sampllng rate.
    6. Reagents
         6.1 Hydrogen peroxide <H202> solutlon, 3%. Prepare a
    solutlon of 3% by volume hydrogen peroxide In dlsttlled water.
         6.2 Cadmium hydroxide (Cd(OH> 2 >. Mix 4.3 g cadmium sulfate
    hydrate (3CdS0 4 .eH 2 0> and 0.3g of sodium hydroxide CNaOH) In
    I lfter of dlstllled water.
    7. Pre-test Procedures
            7.1 Add 100 ml of 3% H2 o2 to one lmplnger.


-           7.2 Add 100 ml of Cd(OH)2 to each of three lmplngers.
            7.3 Assemble the semplfng train es shown In Figure IV-84.
         7.4 Leak-test the sampllng train by starting the sample
    pump, plugging the probe, end adjusting the pump Inlet vacuum
    to 10 Inches Hg. The leak rate must not exceed 0.02 CFM through
    the meter. Before stopping the pump, carefully release the
    plug In the sample probe to avoid backflow of the lmpfnger
    solution.
         7.5 Record the Initial dry test meter reeding and barometric
    pressure as shown In Flgu~e IV-85.
    e. Sempllng

    testing emlss•ons from continuous operations. Each test run
    at batch process operations shall be for 90% of the ~atch time
    or thirty minutes, whichever Is less.    If the yellow color of
    cadmium ~ulflde·t• visible 1n the third cadmium hydroxide
    lmplnger discontinue sampling.
            8.2 Position the probe at the sampllng point and stert the
    pump.

during the test es determined by the rotemeter. Use the

     8.~ Record the followlng lnfoM11atlon at five-minute
Intervals:
     dry test meter temperature
     lmplnger outlet temperature
     dry test meter reading
     8.5 Add Ice as necessary to maintain lmplnger temperatures
et 7°c or less.
     8.6 At the conclusion of eech run, stop the pump, remove
the probe from the stack, end record the fine! meter reeding.
     8.7 Teke three consecutive samples.

     9.1 Disconnect the lmplnger treln from the probe end purge
the treln with clean ambient elr for 15 minutes to ensure ell
    H2S Is removed from the hydrogen peroxide.

                                           Polley and Procedures
          I. Introduction
         • Tfte purpose of this Volume of Manual of Procedures Is to advise
           persons subject to the Regulations of the Bay Area Air Quality
           Mtnagement District of the requirements which must be met by
           comtlnuous emission monitoring lnstallatlons.
         This volume Is en elaboration of the requirements In the Regulations
                and has been adopted by the Board of Directors of this District.
         Persons fall Ing to comply with Its provisions wll I be subject to
         penalty action as provided for In the Health end Safety Code of the
         State of Cellfornla.
         2. App I I ca_b i IJ..!.y.
         The procedures set forth in this Volume ere applicable to the
         requirements for those monitors specified by District regulations.
         Generally, these regulations are designed to fulfill the requirements
         of State and Federal Law, I.e., Sections 42700 of the Cal lfornla
         Health and Safety Code along with Cal lfornla Air Resources Board
         Resolution 75-59 end Title 40, U.S. Code of Federal Regulations,
         Part SI, Appendix P. and Part 60.

(        These documents are available to the publ le. Persons subject to
         these monitoring requirements are encouraged to obtain them and review
         their contents.
         3.          Instrumentation.
                         3.1 General

         The Air Pollutlon Control Officer CAPCO) shall approva plans and
         specifications for monitor selection and placement.
         In al I cases where gaseous monitors are Installed It wll I be necessary
         to determine mass emission rates. Flow rate may be determined
         either by Install Ing a stack gas velocity monitor or by calcu1atlons.
         Any such calculations must be clear, easy to verify, and of
         demonstrated rellablllty.
         All monitors on sources subject to an emission standard shal I be
         equipped with an analog chart recorder. Chart speeds shall be such
         that vlolatlons of the standard are readtly discernible •
          .. ~ Ill



         The Instrument span shall be two to four times the anticipated stack
         concentration. If necessary to provide a clear record of any
         violations, the Instruments shall be dual range with automatic
         attenuation and an event marker to Indicate span on the chart
         recorder.

    \.


-
In certain s.ltuations It may be possible to use a sing le monitor
on a combination of sources or, In the case of an extractive
;ystem, to u~e one monitoring system on multiple sources. The
APCO wll I approve any such appllcatlons on a case-by-case basis.
         3.2 Specifications
Factors taken Into consideration when the APCO eYaluates a
proposed monitor lnstallatlon will be the performance speci-
f lc:::atlons shown In Appendlcles A, B, and C, ff appl lcable, and
the state of the art.
         3.3 Placement
The monitoring locatlon shall be such that a representative sample
Is obtalnable. The APCO may provide alternates to these requirements
In cases of hardship or physlcal lmposslblllty.
4 • I n st a II at I on -Sch e du I es
Occaslonally additional monitors may be required by written
notification from the APCO. For the purpose of lnstallatfon
scheduling, the effective date shall be the date of the letter
from the APCO.
The fol I owing timetable shal I apply to such fns·tal la1"1ons.
         4.1 Submittal of Plans and Specifications
A person shal I respond to the Air Pollut.fon Control Officer In
writing within 45 days from the date of notification that a
monitor Is required. Such response shall Include the plans and
specifications of monitor selection and placement and shall Include
a descriptive brochure from the manufacturer containing performance
specifications and an engineering drawing depicting the placement.
         4.2 Proof of Intent~~ Purchase
Within 45 days of the date :f notification by APCO of approval of
plans and specifications, e person shall furnish the Air Pollutlon
Control Officer documentary proof of Intent to purchase, In the
form of a copy of a purchase order.
         4.3 Completfon of lnstallatlon
Within 180 days of submission of Intent to Purchase the fnstal latlon
t~ prellmlnary fleld callbratlon shall have been completed; and
the Air Pol lutlon Control Officer so notified.

              4.4 Completfon of Testfng Requfrements
         Wfthfn 45 days of completion of fnstallatlon, all requfred tests
         shall have been completed ~nd reported to the APCO.  It may be
         preferable that part of the required testing be done by the
         ,anufacturer at the factory.

         •    4.5 Time Extensions
         Tl•• extensions may be granted at the dfscretlon of the APCO.
         5. Operation
              5.1 General
        Jnstru•ent ■ al functions are to be reported on the monthly report
        lndfcatlng times, type and a brief description of the repairs.
        Periods of fnoperatfon greater than 24 hours must be reported by
        phone to the District dispatcher by the following working day,
        followed by notification of completfon of repafrs. Repefrs must
        be •ade as soon as possible. Downtimes In excess of tffteen days
        shall be deemed a fallure to monftor.

        A record shall be maintained for each monftor describing mafntenance,
        calfbratfon and fnspectlon.
~· ..
r       The Source Test Section of this Ofstrfct wf II conduct periodic,
        Field Accuracy tests to assure proper maintenance and accuracy.
             5.2 Callbratlon
        Dally callbratfon and span checks shal I be made as recommended by
        the manufacturer, except for velocity sensfng fnstrumentatfon which
        shall be cal fbrated on a monthly basis. Daffy records shat I be
        kept and adjustments shall be made ff the drift Is greater than
        ten percent of the appl feeble emission standard or, ff none applies,
        ten percent of span.
         Calibration gases shat I be traceable to NBS standards
         where such reference gases are available. Every three months
         from the date of manufacture, gases .must be re-analyzed by the
         reference methods Indicated In the Appendfcfes, unless the manu­
         facturer guarantees a longer shelf life. Gases should not be
         used after their stated shelf llfe has expired. For extractive
        ~ystems, the callbratlon gases must be Introduced as close to the
        ~Ip of the probe as possfbfe.
             5.3.Malntenance
        Al I monitoring systems shall be ■ afntalned In a good state of
        repair. At the discretion of the APCO, either complete performence

spectftcetion tests or fle)d eccurecy tests may be required after

6. Testing Requirements
Testing requirements depend on the type of monitor end source.
Pefformence tests shel I be conducted et the.completion 9f lnstelletlon
end preliminary field eel ?brettons. A copy of the test results
shil I be furnished to the APCO end one copy shal I be retelned In
owners ft I e.
          6.1 Performance Speclflcetton Tests

Performence Specification Tests ere detetled tests designed to
Insure that en Instrument meets performance specifications for
eccurecy, zero end spen drift, response time end stebll tty.
These tests ere required on the fol lowing monitors on Indicated
sources.

          6.2 Field Accuracy Tests
Fteld Accuracy Tests ere e portion of the Performance Speclflcetlon
Test which ere designed to Insure the accuracy of the monitoring
system. They ere eppltceble to ell systems except trensmtssometers.
The eccurecy requirement ts that the monitoring system must be
within ±20% or 10% of the appllceble emission stenderd, whichever ts
greater, when compared to the Field Accuracy Test Procedure. This
means that not only the component monitors, but also, the result~nt
mass emission rate must meet this specification.
Fteld Accuracy Test Procedure ts shown es Appendix D.

    8. Re-ports

          8.1 Performance Specification Test Report

    This test Is to be conducted by the person responslble for the
    lnstellatlon. One copy of the test results Is to be furnished
    to the APCO upon completion. The APCO wlll notify the person
    of the acceptability of the report.
    4     8.2 Ff e Id Accuracy Test Report
    T-tlfs test may be conducted erther by the person responslbfe for
    rie •onftor or the APCO. Coples of the test results ere to be
    furnrshed by either party to the other upon compfetlon of the test.
          8.3 Report of Excesses
    Any Jndlcated excess of any emission standard to which the st8tlonery
    source rs required to conform, as Indicated by the monitoring
    device, shall be reported by tt~~operator of the source by
    telephone to the District dispatcher within 96 hours after such
    occurrence. The report should Include the nature, extent, cause
    and corrective action taken.
    For the purpose of determining en lndrcated excess rn the case of
    a gaseous pollutant an excess wlf f be considered to have occurred
    If the average of any clock hour exceeds the standard, except for
    start-up of sulfuric acrd plants. Prior to July 31, 1981, sulfur
    dioxide emissions during start-up of suffurlc acid plants shall
    be averaged over the first six hours of operation for purposes of
    determining compliance. After July 31, 1981, the start-up

-   averaging tl~e shall be four hours.
    In the case of opacity, an Indicated excess wll I be considered to
    have occurred rt any cumulatrve period over three minutes within
    any clock hour was In excess of the opacity standard.


    Where computerized date processing systems ere used the APCO
    may approve alternate requirements.
          8.4 Monthly Monltorrng Report
    The data from monitors requrred by this District wlll be used as
    proof of violations of applicable emission standards, to relate
    sources to ground level monitoring excesses and provide emission
    Inventory Information. Therefore, the emissions to be reported
    wlll very with the type of Monitor. The following Is a fist of
    tbe
     __., type of emissions that must be reported for each pollutant:
    Po I I utent   Emlssron Tyoe
    Opacrty        Excesses only
    NOx            Mess Emission Rate (Ton/day) plus NOx concentretron

                     PERFORMANCE SPECIFICATION    . TEST" 1


     Perfor.nance Specification 1 - Perfonnance specifications and
     specification test procedures for transmissometer systems
     for continuous measurement of the- opacity of stack emissions.
     1. Principle and Applicability
      ..  1.1 Principle. The c;,pacity of particulate matter ill
     stack emissions is measured by a continuously operating
     *ssion measurement system. These systems are based upon
     the principle of transmissometry which is a direct measurement
     of the attenuation of visible radiation_ (opacity) by particulate
     matter in a stack effluent. Light having specific spectral
     characteristics is projected from a lamp across the stack
     of a pollutant source to a light sensor. '?he light is
     attenuated due to absorption and scatter by the particulate
     matter in the effluent. 'l'he percentage of visible light
     attenuated is defined as the opacity of the emission.
     Transparent stack emissions that do not attenuate light will
     have a transmittance of 100 or an opacity of O. Opaque
     stack emissions that attenuate all of the visible light
     will have a trans:nittance of O or an opacity of 100 percent.
     The t:ansmissometer is evaluated by use of neutral density
     filters to determine the precision of the continuous
     monitoring system. Tests of the system are perfonned to
     determine zero drift, and response time characteristecs
(
     of the system.
          1.2 Applicability. This perfor:nance specification is
     applicable to the continuous monitoring systems specified in
     the subparts for measuring opacity of emissions. Specifi­
     cation for continuous measurement of visible emissions are
     given in tel:1lls of design, performance, and installation
     parameters, installation requirements, and data compu­
     tation procedures for evaluating the acceptability of the
     continuous monitoring systems subject to approval by the
    APCO.

    2•   Apparatus
           2.1 Calibrated Filters. Optical filters with neutral
      spectral characteristics and known optical densities to
      visible light of screens known to produce specified optical
      densities. Calibrated filters+with accuracies certified
      by the manufacturer to within - 3 percent opacity shall be
      used. Filters required are low, mid, and high-range filters
      with nominal optical densities as follows when the trans­
      apmeter is spanned at opacity levels specified by applicable

3. Definitions.
     3.1 Continuous Monitoring System. ~e total equipment
required for the determination of pollutant opacity in a
source effluent. - Continuous monitoring systems consist of
major subsystems as follows:
 •   3.1.l Sampling ~terface. 'l'he portion of a continuous
monitoring system for opacity that protects the analyzer from
the effluent.
     3.1.2 Analyzer. That portion of the continuous monitoring
system which senses the pollutant and generates a signal output
that is a function of the pollutant opacity.                    ·
      3.1.3 Data Recorder. 'l'hat portion of the continuous
monitoring system that processes the analyzer output and provides
a pe:=manent record of the output signal in terms of pollutant
opacity.
     3.2 Transmissometer. 'l'he portions of a·continuous monitoring
system for opacity that include the sampling interface and the
analyzer.
     3.3 Span. The value of opacity at which the continuous
monitori?lg ·system· is ·set to produce· the maxi.mum· data-display.
output. The span shall be set at an opacity specified in each
applicable subpart.
     3.4 Calibration Error. 'l'he difference between the opacity
reading indicated by the continuous monitoring system and the
known values cf a series of test standards. For this method
~e test standards are a series of calibrated optical filters or
screens.




                                8
                 3.S Zero Drift. The change in continuous monitoring system
 .......,   output over a stated period of time of normal continuous
            operation when the pollutant concentration at the time of the
            measurements is zero.
                 3.6 calibration Drift. The change in the continuous
            mo!itoring system output over a stated period of time.of normal
            continuous operation when the pollutant· ~ncentration at the
            ~ of the measurements is the same known upscale value.

                 3.7 System Response. The time interval from a step
            change in opacity in the staclc at the input to the continuous
            monitoring system to the time at which 9S percent of the corres­
            ponding final value is reached as displayed on the continuous
            monitoring system data recorder.
                 3.8 Operational Test Period. A minimum period of time
            over which a continuous monitoring system is expected to
            operate within certain perfon,ance specifications without
            unscheduled maintanence, repair, or adjustment.
                 3.9 Transmittance. The fraction of incident light that
            is transmitted through an optical mediam of interest.
                 3.10 Opacity. The fraction of incident light that is
/.          attenuated by an optical medium of interest. Opacity (0)

     4. Installation Specification.
      4.1 Location. Thr trans:mi.ssometer must be located across·
 a section of duct or stack that will provide a particulate
 matter flow throu;-h the optical volume of the transmissometer
 that is representative of the particulate matter flow through
 the duct or-stack. It is recommended that the monitor path­
 length or depth of effluent for the transmissometer include
 the entire diameter of the duct or stack. %n inst.a.llations
 using a shorter pathlength, ext:ra·caution must be·used·in
 determining the measurement location representative of the
 particulate matter flow through the duct or $tack.
                                                                        I
      4. l. l The transmissometer location shall be downstream              .........
 from a.ll particulate control equipment.
     4.1.2 The transmissometer. shall be located as far from
bends and obstructions as p:rac:tica.l.
     4.1.3 A transmissometer that is located in the duct or
stack following.a bend shall be installed in the plane defined
by the bend where possible.
     r.l.4 'l'he transmissometer should be installed in an accessible
location.
     4.1.5 When required by the    APCO       , the owner or
operator of.a source.must-demonstrate that the transmissometer
is located in a section of duct or stack where a representative
particulate matter distributation exists. 'l'he determination
shall be accomplished by examining the opacity profile of the
effluent as a series of positions across the duct or stack
while the plant is in operation at maximum or reduced operating
rates or by other tests acceptable to.the .APCO.     ·
 .
._
     4.2 Slotted Tube. Installations that require the use
of a slotted tube shall use a slotted tube of sufficient
size and blackness so as not to interfere with the free flow
of effluent through the entire optical volume of the trans­
missometer or reflect light into the transmissometer photodetector.
Light reflections may be prevented by using blackened baffles
within the slotted tube to prevent the lamp radiation from
impinging upon the tube walls, by restricting the angle of
projection of the light and the angle of view of the photo•
                                                                        -
                                      10
        /

                 detector assembly to less than the cross-sectional area of the
                 slotted tube, or by other methods. The owner or operator must
                 show that the manufacturer of the monitoring system has used
                 appropriate methods to minimuze light reflections for systems
                 using slotted tubes •

            .    9.2.B Operational Test Period. During the 168-hour
           operational test period, the continuous monitoring system
           shall not require any corrective znaintenance, repair,-replace­
           ment, or adjustment other than that clearly specified as.
           required in the manufacturer's operation and maintenance
           manuals as routine and.expected during~ one-week period.
           If the continuous monitoring system is operated wi-;hin the
           specified performance parameters and does not require
           corrective maintenance, repair, replacement, or adjustment
           other than as specified above during the 168-hour test period,
           the operational test period shall have veen successfully
           concluded. Failure of the continuous monitoring system to
           meet these requirements shall call for a repetition of the
           168-hour test period. Portions of the tests which were satis­
           factorily completed need not be repeated. Failure to meet
           any performance specification(s) shall call for a repetition of
           the one-week operational test period and that specific portion
           of the tests required by paragraph 8 related to demonstrating
           compliance with the failed specification. All maintenance
           and adjustments required shall be recorded. Output readings
           shall be recorded before and a.fter all adjustments •

                            PERFO~CE SPECIFICATION TEST 2

              Perfoniance Specification 2 - Performance specifications and
              specification test procedures for Monitors of so2 and NO·
              from stationary sources.                                x
         ·•
           1. Principle and Applicability.
          =       l.l Principle. The concentration of sulfur dioxide or
          ;oxides of nitrogen pollutants in stack emissions is measured
           by a continuously operating emission measurement system.
           Concurrent with operation of the continuous monitoring system,
           the pollutant concentrations are also measured with reference
           methods (Appendix. A). An average of the continuous monitoring
           system data is computed for each reference method testing period
           and compared to determine the relative accuracy of the continuous
           monitoring system. Other tests of the continuous monitoring
           isystem are also performed to determine calibration error, drift,
           and response characteristics of the system.
                 1.2 Applicability. This perfo~ance specification is
           applicable to evaluation of continuous monitoring systems for
           measurement of nitrogen oxides or sulfur dioxide pollutants.
           Tests specifications contain test procedures, installation
          ·requirements, and data computation procedures for evaluating the
           acceptability of the continuous monitoring systems.
     (                  •
           2. Apparatus.
                 2.1 Calibration Gas Mixtures. Mixtures of known concentratio:
           of pollutant gas in a diluent gas shall be prepared. The pollutant
           gas shall be sulfur dioxide or the appropriate oxide(s) of
           nitrogen specified by paragraph 6 and within subparts. For
           sulfur dioxide gas mixtures, the diluent gas may be air or
           nitrogen. For nitric oxide (NO) gas mixtures, the
                                                <
         • diluent gas shall be oxygen-free ( l0ppm) nit::-ogen, and for
           nitrogen dioxide (N0 ) gas mixtures the diluent gas shall be
           air. Concentrations 2 of approximately SO percent and 90 percent
           of span are required. The 90 percent gas mixture is used to set
           and to check the span and is referred to as the span gas.
                2.2 Zero Ga.s. A gas certified by the manufacturer to
          contain less than l ppm of the pollu~nt gas or ambient air
          may be used.
                    2.3 Equipment for measurement of the pollutant gas concent­
           - ration using the reference method specified in the applicable
         .... standard.
                2.• Data Recorder. Analog chart recorder or other
          suitable device with input voltage range compatible with analyzer
          system output. The resolution of the recorder's data output shall
          be sufficient to allow completion of the test procedures
\.        within the specification.


                                          21
          2.5 Continuous monitoring system for S02 or NOx pollutants
    as applicable.
    3. Definitions.
C         3.1 Continuous Monitoring System. ~e total equipment
    required for the determination of a pollutant gas ·concentration
=   in a source effluent. Continuous monitoring systems consist
    of major subsystems as follows:
           3.1.l San;,ling Interface. That portion of an extractive
    continuous monitoring system that·performs one or more of the
    foloowing operations: acquisiilon, trans'DOrtation, and condit­
    ioning of. .a. smuple..of. th~ .source efflnent:or_ that .portion of
    an in-situ continuous monitoring system that protects the anal­
    yzer from the e.ffluent.
          3.1.2 Analyzer. That portion of the continuous monitorinq
    system.which senses the pollutant gas and generates a signal
    output that is a function of the pollutant concentration.
          3. l. 3 Data Recorder. That portion of the continuous
    monitoring system that provides a permanent record of the
    output signal in terms of concentration units.
          3.2 Span. The value of pollutant concentration at
    which the continuous monitoring system is set to produce the
    maximum data display output. The span shall be set at the
    concentration specified in each applicable subpart.
          3.3 Accuracy (Relative).    'l'he degree of correctness
    with which the continuous monitoring system yields the value
    of gas concentration-of a sample relati:ve ~o the value given.
    by a defined reference method. This accuracy is expressed
    in terms of error. which is the difference between the paired
    concentration measurements expressed as a percentage of the
    mean reference value.
          3.4 Calibration Error. The difference between the
    pollutant.concentration.indicated.by the continuous monitoring.
    system and the known concentration of the test gas mixture.
          3.5 Zero Drift. The change in the continuous monitoring
    system output over a stated period of time of normal continuous
    operation when the pollutant concentration at the time for the
    measurements is zero.
          3.6 calibration Drift. ~e change in the continuous
    monitoring system output over a stated ti.me period of normal
    continuous operations when the pollutant concentration at the
    time of the measurements is the same known upscale value.
          3.7 Response Time. The time interval from a step change
    in pollutant concentration at the imput to.the continuous           -
    monitoring system to the time at which 95 percent of the
    corresponding final value is reached as displayed on the continuous

                               22
             monitoring system data recorder.
                   3.8 Operational Period. A min.iJnum period of time over
             which a measurement system is expected to operate within
             certain performance specifications without unscheduled main­
             tenance, rep~, or adjustment.
         •       3.9 Stratification. A condition identified. by a difference
           in excess of 10 percent between the average concentration in
         = the duct or stack and the concentration at any point more
         - than 1.0 meter from the duct or stack wall.

             4. Installation Specifications. Pollutant continuous monitoring
             systems (so 2 and NOx) shall be installed at a sampling
             location where measurements can be made which are directly
             representative .(4 .l), or which can be corrected so as to
             be representative (4.2) of the total emissions from the affected
             facility. Confo:m.ance with this requirement shall be accomp­
             lished as follows:
                 4.1 Effluent gases may be asst1Jlled to be nonstratified
           if a sampling location eight or more stack diameters (equivalent.
           diameters) downstream of IUJ.Y air in-leakage is s.elected •
         . This assumption and data correction procedures under paragraph
           4.2.1 may not be applied to sampling locations upstream of an
           air preheater in a stream generating facility under Subpart D
           of this part. For sampling locations where effluent gases
    (      are either demonstrated (4.3) or may be assumed to be
           nonstratified (eight diameters), a po.int (extractive systems)
           or path (in-situ systems) of average concentration may be
           monitored.
                    4.2 For sampling locations where effluent gases cannot
             be assumed to be nonstratified (less than eight diameters)
             or have been shown under paragraph 4.3 to be stratified,
             results obtained must be consistently representative (e.g.
             a point of average concentration may shift with load changes)
             or the data generated by sampling at·a point (extractive
             systems) or across a path (in-situ systems) must be corrected
             (4.2.1 and 4.2.2) so as to be representative of the total
             emissions from the affected facility. Conformance with this
             requirement may be accomplished in either of the following
             ways:

                   4.2.1 Installation of a diluent continuous monitoring
             system (0 2 or co 2 as applicable) in accordance with the procedures
        ..:. under paragraph 4.2 of Performance Specification 3 of this
             appendix. If the pollutant and the diluent monitoring systems
             are not of the same type (both extractive or both in-situ),
             the extractive system must use a multipoint probe.
                4.2.2 Installation of extractive pollutant monitoring
          systems using multipoint sampling probes or in-situ pollutant
         .monitoring systems that sample or view emissions which are
'         consistenUy representative of the total emissions for the entire

                                                23
                        ·-·   ·-·---·•-· - -··-- ·----.. ---. ·•-.
           cross section. The Administrator may require data to be
           submitted to demonstrate that the emissions sampled or
           viewed are consistently representative for several typical
           facility process operating conditions •
       •         4.3 The owner or 9perator may perform a traverse to
       =   che.racteri:e any stratification of effluent gases that might
       =   exist in a stack or duct. If no stratification is present,
           sampling procedures under paragraph 4.1 may be applied even
           though the eight diameter criteria is not met.
                4.4-. When single point sampling probes for extractive
           systems are installed within the stack or duct under para­
           graphs ·4 .l ·and -·4·. 2·~1, the· sample 1DllY" not-~be- extracted· -at
           any point less than 1.0 meter from the stack or duct wall.
           Multipoint sampling probes installed under paragraph 4.2.2
           may_be located at any points necessary to obtain cons~stently
           representative samples.

          test procedures shall be requirenemts of paragraphs.

                6.1 Calibration Error Test Procedure. Set up arid
          calibrate the complete continuous monitoring system according
          to the manufacturer's written inst.ructions. This m2.y be
          accomplished either in the laboratory or in the field.
                6.1.1 Calibration Gas Analyses. Triplicate analyses
          of the gas mixtures shall be performed within two weeks
          prior to use using Reference Methods 6 for SO2 and 7 for
          NOx• Analyze each calibration gas mixture (SOI, 90\) and
          record the results on the exampl~ sheet shown in Figure 2-1.
          Each sample test result must be within 20 percent of the
          averaged result or the tests shall be repeated. This step
          may be omitted for non-extractive monitors where dynamic
          calibration gas mixtures are not used. (6.1.2).
/               6.1.2 Calibration Error Test Procedure. Make a total
-         of 15 nonconsecutive measurements by alternately using zero
          gas and each calibration gas mixture concentration (e.g.,
          o,, SO\, o,, 901, SO\, 901, SOI, 01, etc.). For nonextractive
          continuous monitoring systems, this test procedure may be
          performed by using two or more calibration gas cells whose
          concentrations are certified by the manufacturer to be
          functionally equivalent to these gas concentrations. Convert
          the continuous monitoring system output readings to ppm and
          record the results on the example sheet shown in Figure 2-2.
                6.2 Field Test for Accuracy (Relative), Zero Drift,
          and Calibration Drift. Install and operate the continuous
          monitoring system in accordance with the manufacturer's
          written instructions and cravings as follows:
                6.2.1 Conditioning Period. Offset the zero setting at
          least 10 percent of the s~an so that negative zero drift
          can be quantified. Operate the system for an initial 168-hour
     __;. conditioning period in no_rmal operating manner.
                6.2.2 Operational Test Period. _Operate the continuous
          monitoring system for an additional 168-hour period
          retaining the zero offset. The system shall monitor the source
          effluent at all times except when being zeroed, calibrated,
          or backfl.li.shed.

     6.3.2 Response Time Test Procedure. Introduce zero gas Into

reeding has stabilized, switch quickly to a known concentration

to 95 percent of final stable response. For non-extractive
monitors, the highest avalfable calibration gas concentration
shell be switched Into and out of the sample path and response
times recorded. Perform this test sequence three (3) times.
Record the results of each test on the example sheet s~own In

                7.2.l Accuracy (See Appendix D).

                7.2.2 calibration Error. Osing the data from paragraph
          6.1, subtract the measured pollutant concentration determined
        'under paragraph 6.1.l (Figure 2-1) from the value-shown by
          the continuous monitoring system for each of the five readings
        ; at each concentration measured under 6.1.2 (Figure 2-2).
          Calculate the mean of these difference values and the 95
          percent confidence intervals according to equations 2-1 and
          202. Report the calibration error (the sum of the absolute
          value of the mean difference and the 95 percent confidence
          interval) as a percentage of. each respective calibration
          gas concentration. Ose example sheet shown in Figure 2-2.
               7.2.3 Zero Drift (2-hour). Osing the zero concentration
         values measured each two hours during the field test, calculate
         the differences between consecutive two-hour readings expressed
         in ppm. calculate the mean difference and the confidence
         interval using as the sum of the absolute mean value and the
         confidence interval as a percentage of span. Use example
         sheet shown in Figure 2-4.
               7.2.4 Zero Drift (24 Bour). Using the zero concentra~ion
         values measured every 24 hours during the field test, calculate
         the differences between the. zero point after zero adjustment
         and the zero value 24 hours later just prior to zero ad­
         justment. Calculate the mean value of these points and the
         confidence interval using equations 2-l and 2-2. Report the
         zero drift (the sum of t~e absolute mean and confidence
         interval) as a percentage of span. Use example sheet shown
         in Figure 2-5.                    •
                7.2.S Calibration Drift (2-hour). Using the calibration
         values obtained at two-hour intervals during the field test,
         calculate the differences between consecutive two-hour
         readings expressed as ppm. These values should be corrected
         for the corresponding zero drift during that two-hour period.
         Calculate the mean and confidence interval of these corrected
         difference values using equations 2-l and 2-2. Do not use the
         differences between non-consecutive readings. Report the
         calibration drift as the sum of the absolute mean and confidence
         interval as a percentage of span. Ose the example sheet shown
         in Figure 2-4.

        after zero and calibration adjustment, and the calibration·
        concentration reading 24 hours later after zero adjustment
        but before calibration adjustment. Calculate the mean value         --..

                   7.2.8 Operational Test Period. During the 168-hour
            performance and operational test period, the continuous
            monitoring system shall not require any correcti~ maintenance
            repair, replacement, or adjustment other than that clearly
            specified as required in the operation and maintenance manuals
            as routine and expected during a one-week period. If the
            continuous..monitoring system operates within t.."le specified
            performance parameters and does not require corrective .
            maintenance, repair, replacement or adjustment other than
            as specified above during the 168 hour test period, the
            operational period will be successfully concluded. Failure of
            the continuous monitoring system to meet this requirement
            shall call for a repetition of the 168-hour test period.
            Portions of the test which were satisfactorily completed
            need not be repeated. Failure.to meet any performance
            specifications shall call for a repetition of the one-week
            performance test period and that portion of the testing which
            is related to the failed specification. All maintenance
            and adjust:nents-·required··shal.l·· be recorded;· · Output readings
            shall be recorded before and after all adjustments.
